Nikasha: session transcript

How Nikasha was planned, specified, built and deployed with Claude, 14–16 October 2026. Live app: nikasha.vercel.app

Part 1 · The brief (claude.ai)

56 prompts. The planning chat where the idea, scope, name, data, disclaimer and spec structure were decided, ending with the handoff document NIKASHA_BRIEF.md.

Part 2 · Specs, build and deployment (Claude Code)

121 prompts in 7 phases. Spec-first work in VS Code: the specs, development with tests, local testing and feedback, the release review and deployment to Vercel and AWS Lambda.

  • Each card is one prompt. Click a number to jump to it, and open "Claude's response" to read the reply.
  • Prompts and replies are verbatim. Tool calls (commands, file edits, web searches) appear as one-line summaries.
  • Names of other trading products and brokers are replaced with “[another product]”. Private links, email and account identifiers are hidden. Times are UTC.
  • Dashed notes in Part 2 mark where the session reached its context limit and continued from a written summary.

Congrats, that's a useful one to have right now. Spec-driven development has become a practical way to work with AI coding agents: you write the requirements, design, and acceptance criteria up front, and the code gets generated and checked against them.

PROMPT 2026-10-13 22:51 UTC

Now I want to submit a assignment which should be project develop and deploy using same strategy but I am not getting idea like what to build

Claude's response

Pick something small with clear, testable rules. Spec-driven work shines when the requirements are precise, so evaluators can trace each feature back to the spec. Here are a few ideas that fit:

  1. Trade journal & P&L tracker. Log stock trades and calculate realized/unrealized P&L, win rate, and charges like brokerage and STT. Since you trade, you'd write the rules from real knowledge, and the calculations make great acceptance criteria.
  2. Expense splitter (Splitwise-lite). Groups, shared bills, and a "who owes whom" settlement. The settlement logic is a nice spec-and-test exercise.
  3. Appointment booking system for a clinic or salon. Slots, no double-booking, cancellations, and reminders. It's a clean state machine to specify.
  4. URL shortener with analytics. Custom aliases, expiry, and click stats. It's simple and well-scoped, so you could finish it fast.
  5. College event registration. Seat limits, a waitlist that auto-promotes on cancellation, and QR check-in.

My pick is #1. It's personal, rule-heavy, and easy to demo.

Whatever you choose, make sure the submission shows the method, not just the app:

  • A requirements spec with user stories and acceptance criteria (Given/When/Then)
  • A design doc covering data model, APIs, and architecture
  • A task breakdown that traces back to requirements
  • Tests mapped to acceptance criteria
  • A live deployment link plus a README

Which tech stack are you comfortable with, and when is it due? I can draft the full spec for whichever idea you choose.

PROMPT 2026-10-13 22:55 UTC

Can we do a strategy analysis for option trading in nifty. Since Igave you last 10 yrs data and vix data as well so can we build something like people will come up with strategy and after analysing it we will generate the report and suggestions to better the strategy or suggest some alternative more profitable strategy? It can be for option buying or selling only on nifty 50 as we have last 10 yrs of data

Claude's response

Yes, this is a strong project, much better than a generic app. It's rule-heavy and measurable, which suits spec-driven development well. One catch: the data isn't in this conversation, since files from other chats don't carry over. Please attach the Nifty and VIX files here.

Rough shape of the product

  1. Strategy input: a structured form rather than free text. Fields would include buy or sell, CE/PE/both, strike choice (ATM, ±N points, or by delta), entry day and time, stop-loss, target, exit rule, and an optional VIX filter (e.g., "only sell when VIX > 15").
  2. Backtest engine: runs the rules over the 10 years, trade by trade.
  3. Report: P&L curve, win rate, max drawdown, expectancy, worst streak, and results split by VIX regime and by market trend.
  4. Suggestions:
    – Tweak your own parameters (wider SL, a different strike, adding a VIX filter) and show the impact.
    – Compare against standard alternatives: short straddle, iron condor, debit/credit spreads, and plain buying.

The key question is what your data contains

  • Actual option prices (historical option chain): the backtests will be realistic.
  • Only Nifty spot plus VIX: we'd have to estimate option prices with Black-Scholes, using VIX as volatility. That's fine for a project, but the report must clearly say premiums are modelled. This matters most for option buying, where small premium errors change the results a lot.

Also, is the data daily or intraday? A daily file can't test intraday SL/target exits accurately.

The rules also changed over the decade (lot sizes, weekly expiry days), so these become explicit spec items. That's a nice point to show graders. The report should also carry a note that past backtests don't guarantee future returns.

Attach the files and tell me your preferred tech stack. I'll check the data and then write the requirements spec.

4
PROMPT 2026-10-13 22:58 UTC

Okay but let's do this. Let allow user to create the option strategy in the system. They can create their own starttegy then we should conver it in a specific format so that for baktest it will be helpful to provide the report and suggestions. How about this?

Claude's response

Yes, that's the right design, and it's the best part of the project for a spec-driven course. The strategy format becomes the contract between the builder UI, the backtest engine, and the report. You spec it once, validate it strictly, and every other part depends on it.

Flow

  1. Strategy builder (UI): the user picks legs, strikes, entry/exit rules, SL/target, and filters from dropdowns.
  2. Canonical format (JSON): the UI saves the strategy as a validated JSON document.
  3. Backtest engine: reads only that JSON and never the UI, so it stays testable on its own.
  4. Report + suggestions: the engine creates modified versions of the same JSON (a different SL, an added VIX filter) and re-runs them to find improvements.

Example of the format (a short straddle with a VIX filter):

{
  "name": "Weekly short straddle",
  "underlying": "NIFTY50",
  "legs": [
    {"action": "SELL", "type": "CE", "strike": "ATM", "lots": 1},
    {"action": "SELL", "type": "PE", "strike": "ATM", "lots": 1}
  ],
  "entry": {"days_before_expiry": 2, "time": "09:20"},
  "exit": {"stop_loss_pct": 30, "target_pct": 50, "time_exit": "15:15"},
  "filters": {"vix_min": 12, "vix_max": 25}
}

Why this scores well

  • The JSON schema is a precise spec you can show in the submission.
  • Every field gets acceptance criteria. For example, "a strategy with SL > 100% on a sold leg is rejected."
  • Suggestions are just edited JSON that gets re-run, so they're explainable rather than black-box.
  • Optional bonus: let users type a strategy in plain English and convert it to this JSON with an LLM.

For the MVP, I'd limit it to 1–4 legs, strikes as ATM/OTM-by-points, daily-expiry logic, and a VIX filter. Adjustments and rolling can come in v2.

Next step: I can write the full requirements spec (user stories, the JSON schema, acceptance criteria) as a document you can submit. Want me to start, and which stack do you plan to use (e.g., Python/FastAPI + React, or Streamlit)?

5
PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:02 UTC

I would suggest you should allow more than that. You should allow to create strategy with almost all possible parameters user can select. Let me share a sample strategy with you. Now you look and understand how I wanted for user to build their strategy.

Attached: nifty_weekly_options_strategy.pdf
Claude's response

Got it. You want a full rule builder where users assemble any strategy from parts, not a few fixed templates. Your PDF breaks down into 8 building blocks, and the builder should make each one configurable:

  1. Market & timeframe: underlying and candle size (1/3/5/15/60-min, daily).
  2. Indicators: a library (Supertrend, EMA, SMA, RSI, ADX, MACD, VWAP, Bollinger…), each with its own settings. Users can add several of the same kind, like EMA 9 and EMA 21.
  3. Entry conditions: rows of [left] [operator] [right], joined with AND/OR, with separate sets for CE and PE. Operators would include >, <, crosses above/below, turns (state flip), and slope rising over N candles.
  4. Instrument: buy or sell, CE/PE, strike by points ITM/OTM, by delta, or by premium, and the expiry (weekly, next week, monthly).
  5. Exits:
    – SL by trigger-candle high/low on the premium chart, fixed points, or %, with a cap
    – Target by risk-reward ratio or fixed points
    – Trailing (to cost at 1R, or stepped)
    – Exit after N candles, or at a hard time
  6. Position sizing: base lots plus VIX-band rules.
  7. Session filters: no-trade windows and an expiry-day cutoff.
  8. Daily limits: max trades, stop after N SL hits, max daily loss.

The CE side of your strategy in the JSON format:

{
  "timeframe": "15m",
  "indicators": {
    "st": {"type": "SUPERTREND", "period": 10, "mult": 2},
    "ema9": {"type": "EMA", "period": 9},
    "ema21": {"type": "EMA", "period": 21},
    "rsi": {"type": "RSI", "period": 14},
    "adx": {"type": "ADX", "period": 14}
  },
  "entry_CE": {"all": [
    ["st", "turns", "GREEN"],
    ["ema9", ">", "ema21"],
    ["close", ">", "ema9"], ["close", ">", "ema21"],
    ["rsi", ">", 55],
    {"any": [["adx", ">", 30],
             {"all": [["adx", ">", 20], ["adx", "slope_up", 1]]}]}
  ]},
  "instrument": {"action": "BUY", "type": "CE", "strike": "ITM_100"},
  "exit": {"sl": "trigger_candle_low_premium", "sl_cap_pts": 35,
           "target_rr": 1.5, "trail_to_cost_at_rr": 1,
           "max_candles": 4},
  "sizing": {"vix_bands": [[0, 12, 0.5], [12, 18, 1.0], [18, 99, 0.5]]},
  "session": {"no_entry": ["11:00-13:30"], "expiry_day_cutoff": "12:00"},
  "daily": {"max_trades": 2, "stop_after_sl_hits": 2}
}

Your PDF also shows why the spec matters. A backtest can't run on vague rules, so the builder has to force each of these into a number:

  • "Closes decisively above" needs a threshold: by how many points or %?
  • SL cap "30–35" needs to be one value.
  • VIX > 18 "reduce significantly" needs a %. I used 50% as a placeholder.
  • "Turns green" could mean only the flip candle, or any candle while it's green.
  • "Thursday" should become "expiry day," because Nifty's weekly expiry weekday has changed over the decade.

Pointing these out in your report is great evidence of spec-driven thinking.

Data check: this strategy needs 15-minute Nifty candles, plus option premium candles for the trigger-candle SL. What exactly is in your 10-year data: daily or intraday, and spot only or option prices too? That decides whether we can test premium-based rules directly or need to model them.

6
PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:06 UTC

I have 10 yrs data for every minute so its not an issue. Now what you suggest how to ask for strategy from user to build out strategy builder followed by backtest and suggetion and improvement with data if required

Claude's response

I'd offer three ways to enter a strategy, all ending in the same JSON format:

  1. Guided builder (main path): a step-by-step wizard with one screen per block: timeframe → indicators → CE/PE entry conditions → strike → exits → sizing → session filters → daily limits. Each step uses dropdowns and number fields, so nothing is vague.
  2. Plain English or PDF upload: the user pastes text or uploads a document like yours. An LLM converts it to JSON and pre-fills the builder.
  3. Templates: start from a ready strategy (Supertrend buying, short straddle, iron condor) and edit it.

The key step is a clarification screen. Paths 2 and 3 often produce gaps, so before any backtest the system flags vague or missing rules and asks targeted questions. For your PDF, it would ask:

  • "Decisively above EMA = by how many points?"
  • "SL cap: 30 or 35?"

Nothing runs until every field is precise. This is spec-driven development built into the product itself, which is a strong point for your assignment.

The full flow after that

  1. Validate: schema checks and logic checks (e.g., target must exceed zero, no-trade window must fall inside market hours).
  2. Backtest: your minute data gets resampled to whatever timeframe the strategy uses, so 1m, 5m, 15m and 1h all work.
  3. Report:
    – Equity curve, win rate, drawdown, expectancy
    – Breakdowns by year, by VIX band, by weekday or expiry day, and CE vs PE
  4. Suggestions:
    – Tune: test nearby values (SL 25/30/35, RSI 50/55/60) and show what changes.
    – Filter: find where the strategy loses (e.g., VIX < 12, Mondays) and propose excluding it.
    – Alternatives: run comparable strategies on the same period and show which did better.
  5. Accept and compare: the user applies a suggestion, it's saved as version 2, and the two versions are shown side by side.

One rule to put in the spec early: guard against overfitting. Tuning on 10 years and reporting on the same 10 years will always look great. Instead, optimize on the first ~7 years and report results on the last ~3 years the tuning never saw. Graders will notice this.

One data check: is your minute data Nifty spot only, or option premiums too? Your PDF's SL uses the option premium chart, so this decides whether premiums are read from the data or estimated.

Want me to write the requirements spec next (user stories, acceptance criteria, the JSON schema) as a document you can submit?

7
PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:09 UTC

I have only nifty 50 spot data. No option premium data

Claude's response · 1 action
  • Searched the web: Nifty weekly expiry day history Thursday Tuesday change weekly options launch 2019

That works. We'll model option premiums from spot, which is a standard approach when you don't have option data. The report just has to be honest that premiums are estimated.

How premiums get estimated

For every minute, the engine calculates the option price with Black-Scholes using:

  • Nifty spot at that minute
  • Strike (e.g., ATM ± 100)
  • India VIX as the volatility input
  • Time left to expiry, in minutes
  • A risk-free rate (fixed, e.g., ~6.5%)

Building premium candles from these minute prices gives your PDF's "low of the trigger candle on the premium chart" a real number to work with.

Known gaps, written into the spec as assumptions

  • Skew and expiry-day IV: VIX is a single 30-day volatility figure. Real weekly options near expiry (and OTM strikes) price differently, so results are least reliable on expiry day.
  • Costs: add configurable slippage, brokerage and taxes per trade so results aren't flattering.
  • Expiry calendar: this has to be a dated table, not a fixed weekday:
    – NSE introduced weekly Nifty options in 2019, so earlier years only had monthly expiries. Weekly strategies should start from 2019.
    – Nifty expiry was Thursday until contracts expiring from 1 September 2025, when it moved to Tuesday.
  • Lot sizes: also a dated table, since they've changed over the years.

A design point that will impress graders: put pricing behind an interface (PricingProvider). Ship BlackScholesVIXProvider now, and leave room for RealOptionDataProvider later. The backtest engine never knows which one it's using. That's clean, spec-first architecture.

Also, every report should show a "Premiums modelled, not actual" badge plus a sensitivity check: rerun with VIX ± 2 points. If the strategy only works within that narrow band, it's fragile.

Is your VIX data minute-level or daily? Either works, but minute-level gives more realistic intraday premiums. After that, I can write the full requirements spec.

Sources:

  • Nifty Weekly Options Trading Dynamics, equitiesindia.com
  • Weekly F&O Expiry, Navia

PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:13 UTC

For Nifty 50, i have 1-minute candles from 9 Jan 2015 to 15 May 2026, and same for India vix

Claude's response

That's ideal: 1-minute Nifty and VIX for 11+ years is enough to build this properly, and minute-level VIX makes the modelled premiums much more realistic.

How the data maps to the spec

  • Weekly strategies: backtest from 2019, when weekly Nifty options began, to 15 May 2026. That's about 7 years.
  • Monthly strategies: can use the full range from Jan 2015.
  • Overfitting guard, using the weekly window:
    – Tune on 2019–2023
    – Report results on Jan 2024–May 2026, which the tuning never sees
  • Data checks to run once at load:
    – Missing minutes
    – Holidays and special sessions (e.g., Muhurat trading)
    – Nifty and VIX timestamps lining up
    – Unusual spikes

The engine should log and skip bad days, not crash. These checks become acceptance criteria too.

All the inputs are now settled: minute spot, minute VIX, modelled premiums, and a dated expiry and lot-size calendar.

Next is the requirements spec: user stories, the strategy JSON schema, acceptance criteria, the pricing assumptions, and the data rules, as a document you can submit. Before I write it, which stack will you build with? For example, Python + FastAPI + React, or Python + Streamlit (faster to build and deploy). If you're unsure, I'd suggest Streamlit for an assignment.

PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:15 UTC

See since this is the project i have to submit what you suggest? I have vercel account to deploy

Claude's response · 1 action
  • Searched the web: Vercel functions limits max duration Hobby Python runtime bundle size 2026

Since you have Vercel, I'd suggest Next.js (React) for the UI plus Python serverless functions for the backtest engine, in one repo and one Vercel deploy. Streamlit is out, because it needs an always-on server, which Vercel doesn't provide.

Why this fits

  • Vercel's free tier is enough:
    – Python functions get up to 500 MB uncompressed
    – Hobby gives 2 GB of memory
    – Hobby allows up to 300 seconds per function call
  • Python is the right tool for the engine: pandas and numpy make indicators, resampling and Black-Scholes fast. One backtest over 7 years of minute data should take seconds.
  • The spec becomes code: one JSON Schema file for the strategy format, validated in the UI (TypeScript, with zod or ajv) and in the engine (pydantic). The same contract is enforced on both sides, which is spec-driven development in practice.

Making it work within Vercel's limits

  • Data: convert your CSVs to Parquet once. 11 years of minutes is about 1M rows per series, probably 20–40 MB, and gets bundled with the function. Deploy via GitHub rather than the CLI, since Hobby limits CLI uploads to 100 MB of source files.
  • Suggestions: run each variant (SL 25/30/35, a VIX filter, an alternative strategy) as a separate API call, in parallel from the browser. No single call comes near the time limit. Cap sweeps at around 20–30 variants.
  • Storage (MVP): save strategies in the browser, with JSON export/import. No database needed for the assignment.
  • Bonus, if time allows: a plain-English or PDF-to-JSON converter using an LLM API.

Suggested build order

  1. Spec + JSON Schema
  2. Data loader and checks
  3. Backtest engine with tests mapped to acceptance criteria
  4. Report API
  5. Strategy builder UI
  6. Suggestions
  7. Deploy
  8. README with the live link

Shall I write the requirements spec now? It would cover user stories, the strategy schema, acceptance criteria, pricing assumptions, data rules and this architecture, as a document you can submit and build from.

Sources:

  • Vercel Functions Limits
  • Vercel Limits overview

PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:18 UTC

Thanks for now. Will comeback. But for now just give me a scope statement so that I can go to claude code and start there conversation

Claude's response

Here's a scope statement you can paste straight into Claude Code:

PROJECT: Nifty 50 Options Strategy Builder, Backtester & Advisor
Course assignment (spec-driven development). Must be built spec-first and deployed on Vercel.

GOAL
A web app where users build a Nifty 50 option buying/selling strategy using almost any
parameters, backtest it on historical data, get a performance report, and receive
data-backed suggestions to improve it or switch to a better-performing alternative.

DATA (provided, in /data)
- Nifty 50 spot: 1-minute OHLC, 9 Jan 2015 – 15 May 2026
- India VIX: 1-minute, same period
- No option premium data: premiums must be MODELLED (see Pricing)

IN SCOPE (MVP)
1. Strategy builder (guided wizard), covering:
   - Timeframe (1/3/5/15/30/60-min, daily), resampled from 1-min data
   - Indicator library with user parameters, multiple instances allowed
     (Supertrend, EMA, SMA, RSI, ADX, MACD, VWAP, Bollinger Bands)
   - Entry conditions as [left][operator][right] rows with nested AND/OR groups;
     operators: >, <, >=, <=, crosses above/below, turns (state flip),
     slope up/down over N candles; separate CE and PE condition sets
   - Instrument: BUY/SELL, CE/PE, strike by ATM ± points / delta / premium,
     expiry (current weekly, next weekly, monthly); 1–4 legs
   - Exits: SL (trigger-candle high/low on premium, fixed points, %), SL cap,
     target (R:R or fixed), trail to cost at xR, exit after N candles, hard exit time
   - Position sizing: base lots + VIX-band multipliers
   - Session filters: no-entry time windows, expiry-day cutoff time
   - Daily limits: max trades/day, stop after N SL hits, max daily loss
2. Canonical strategy format: one JSON Schema = single source of truth,
   validated in both UI (TypeScript) and engine (Python/pydantic)
3. Clarification step: flag vague or missing values; no backtest until the
   strategy fully validates
4. Templates: a few ready strategies the user can edit
5. Backtest engine with:
   - Configurable slippage, brokerage and taxes
   - Dated expiry calendar and lot-size tables (never hardcode a weekday)
6. Report:
   - Equity curve, win rate, max drawdown, expectancy, worst losing streak
   - Breakdowns by year, VIX band, weekday/expiry day, CE vs PE
7. Suggestions engine:
   - Parameter sweeps
   - Loss-regime filters (e.g., exclude VIX < 12)
   - Comparison with standard alternative strategies
   Suggestions are edited strategy JSON, re-run and shown side by side as versions
8. Overfitting guard:
   - Weekly strategies: tune on 2019–2023, report on Jan 2024 – May 2026
   - Monthly strategies may use data from 2015

PRICING (modelled premiums)
- Black-Scholes per minute: spot, strike, India VIX as IV, minutes to expiry,
  fixed risk-free rate (configurable)
- Pricing behind a PricingProvider interface: BlackScholesVIXProvider now,
  RealOptionDataProvider possible later
- Every report shows a "Premiums modelled, not actual" badge plus a VIX ± 2
  sensitivity rerun

DATA RULES
- Weekly Nifty options start in 2019; earlier years are monthly-only
- Nifty weekly expiry was Thursday until contracts expiring from 1 Sep 2025, then Tuesday
- Load-time checks: missing minutes, holidays/special sessions, Nifty–VIX
  timestamp alignment, outlier spikes; log and skip bad days, never crash
- Convert CSV to Parquet at build time

ARCHITECTURE & DEPLOYMENT
- Next.js (React, TypeScript) frontend + Python serverless functions (pandas/numpy),
  one repo, deployed to Vercel Hobby via GitHub
- Each backtest/variant is a separate API call (stay well under the 300s limit);
  suggestion variants run in parallel from the browser, max ~30 per sweep
- Storage: browser storage + JSON export/import (no database in MVP)

OUT OF SCOPE (MVP)
Live trading or broker integration, real option-chain data, user accounts,
adjustments/rolling of positions, instruments other than Nifty 50.
Optional bonus: plain-English/PDF → strategy JSON via an LLM.

SUCCESS CRITERIA
- Public Vercel URL; a user can build, validate, backtest and improve a strategy end to end
- Spec docs: requirements.md (user stories + Given/When/Then acceptance criteria),
  design.md, tasks.md, strategy.schema.json
- Automated tests mapped to acceptance criteria
- README with setup, assumptions, limitations and a disclaimer
  (backtests are not a guarantee of future returns)

HOW TO WORK
Do NOT write application code yet. First ask me clarifying questions, then produce
requirements.md, design.md, tasks.md and strategy.schema.json for my review.
Use my sample strategy (15-min Supertrend/EMA/RSI/ADX ITM option buying) as the
first worked example the schema must express.

When you start in Claude Code, also share your PDF strategy there so it can use it as the test case. Good luck with the build!

PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:20 UTC

You have given a lot. Can you just give a problem statement and then I will start from there

Claude's response

Here's a short problem statement you can paste:

PROBLEM STATEMENT

Retail traders in Nifty 50 options often trade strategies built on indicators,
rules and gut feel, without knowing how those strategies would have performed
historically. Testing a strategy properly today requires coding skills,
historical option data, and a lot of time, so most traders skip it and risk
real money on untested ideas.

Build a web application where a trader can define their own Nifty 50 option
buying or selling strategy, using almost any parameters (timeframe, indicators,
entry conditions, strike selection, stop-loss, target, trailing, time filters,
VIX-based sizing, daily limits). The system converts it into a standard,
validated strategy format and backtests it on 1-minute Nifty 50 and India VIX
data from 2015 to 2026, with option premiums modelled from spot and VIX.

The system then generates a clear performance report and suggests
data-backed improvements or better-performing alternative strategies.

The project must be built using specification-driven development and deployed
on Vercel.

Paste this into Claude Code and ask it to start by asking you clarifying questions. You can share the detailed scope from earlier when it asks for specifics.

13
PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:22 UTC

But do you think it's a good project?

Claude's response

Yes, it's a strong project, and well matched to a spec-driven course. Just keep the scope under control.

Why it's good

  • It needs a precise spec to work at all. Vague trading rules can't be backtested, so the spec is visibly the heart of the product, not a formality.
  • It's genuinely useful and personal. You trade, so you can judge whether the output makes sense. Graders like real problems over to-do apps.
  • It has clear, testable acceptance criteria. Indicator values, P&L math and validation rules all make concrete tests.
  • It shows engineering judgment: modelling premiums honestly, guarding against overfitting, handling the expiry-day change.

The real risk is scope. The "almost any parameter" builder plus suggestions is a lot for one assignment. To stay safe:

  • Get one complete path working first: build → validate → backtest → report → deploy. Your PDF strategy is the perfect first test case.
  • Add more indicators, operators and the suggestions engine only after that works end to end.
  • Treat the LLM text-to-strategy converter as a bonus, not core.

A smaller app that is fully specified, tested and deployed will score better than an ambitious one that's half-finished. If you keep that discipline, this is easily above-average for a course submission.

14
PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:23 UTC

How one can use who don't know about market?

Claude's response

Honestly, as designed, it's built for traders. Someone who doesn't know the market wouldn't know what Supertrend, ITM strikes or stop-losses mean, so they couldn't build a meaningful strategy.

It can still be useful to beginners if you frame it as a learning tool: "see how a strategy would have performed before risking real money." A beginner mode could add:

  • Template gallery with plain explanations: for example, "This strategy buys a call when the trend turns up and exits if it loses ₹30." Users pick one and run it without building anything.
  • Tooltips and a glossary: every field explains itself in one line (what VIX is, what ITM means).
  • Plain-English report summary: "This strategy made money in 4 of 7 years, but lost heavily when markets were calm. A ₹1 lakh account would have dropped to ₹62,000 at its worst point."
  • What-if sliders: change one thing, like the stop-loss, and see how results move. Learning by experimenting.
  • Risk-first framing: show worst drawdown and losing streaks as prominently as profit, plus a clear note that backtests don't guarantee future results.

The tool shouldn't present itself as "find me a profitable strategy" for beginners. Options can lose money fast, and the most valuable thing it can teach a newcomer is why most strategies fail.

For your assignment, keep traders as the primary user and add one or two beginner user stories (templates plus the plain-English summary). That shows you thought about different users without expanding the scope much.

15
PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:24 UTC

This is good idea. Also can we give a sample for beginners to try?

Claude's response

Yes. A good beginner sample is simple, with few rules, and each rule explainable in one sentence. Here's one:

"Simple Trend Follower" (beginner template)

In plain words: when the short-term average price crosses above the longer-term average, the market may be starting to rise, so buy a Call. When it crosses below, buy a Put. Exit at a fixed loss or profit, and never hold overnight.

RuleSettingWhat it means
Chart15-minute candlesEach candle = 15 minutes of price movement
Buy Call when9 EMA crosses above 21 EMAShort-term trend turns up
Buy Put when9 EMA crosses below 21 EMAShort-term trend turns down
StrikeATM (nearest to Nifty price)The most commonly traded option
Stop-loss30% of premiumBought at ₹100? Exit if it falls to ₹70
Target50% of premiumExit if it rises to ₹150
Entry window09:30 – 14:30Avoid the noisy open and late entries
Force exit15:15No overnight risk
Daily limitMax 2 tradesPrevents overtrading
Size1 lotSmallest possible position

The same template in the strategy format:

{
  "name": "Simple Trend Follower",
  "level": "beginner",
  "timeframe": "15m",
  "indicators": {
    "ema9": {"type": "EMA", "period": 9},
    "ema21": {"type": "EMA", "period": 21}
  },
  "entry_CE": {"all": [["ema9", "crosses_above", "ema21"]]},
  "entry_PE": {"all": [["ema9", "crosses_below", "ema21"]]},
  "instrument": {"action": "BUY", "strike": "ATM", "expiry": "current_weekly", "lots": 1},
  "exit": {"sl_pct": 30, "target_pct": 50, "time_exit": "15:15"},
  "session": {"entry_window": ["09:30-14:30"]},
  "daily": {"max_trades": 2}
}

Why it works as a beginner sample

  • Only two indicators and one condition per side, easy to understand.
  • The report will likely show a key lesson clearly: crossover strategies do well in trending markets and lose repeatedly in sideways ones. The VIX and year breakdowns will make that visible.
  • It's a natural starting point for the what-if sliders: "What if the stop-loss were 20%?"

You could add two more templates later: a "Higher-Timeframe Trend" version (1-hour candles, fewer trades) and a non-directional one (for example, a simple short straddle) to show beginners that selling options behaves very differently from buying them.

PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:25 UTC

Claude's response

Here are both, updated with the beginner requirements.

Problem statement

PROBLEM STATEMENT

Retail traders in Nifty 50 options often trade strategies built on indicators,
rules and gut feel, without knowing how those strategies would have performed
historically. Testing a strategy properly requires coding skills, historical
option data and a lot of time, so most traders skip it and risk real money on
untested ideas. Beginners face an even bigger gap: they don't understand the
terminology, and have no safe way to learn how strategies behave or why most
of them fail.

Build a web application that serves two kinds of users:
- Experienced traders define their own Nifty 50 option buying or selling
  strategy using almost any parameters (timeframe, indicators, entry conditions,
  strike selection, stop-loss, target, trailing, time filters, VIX-based sizing,
  daily limits).
- Beginners start from ready-made templates explained in plain language, run
  them without building anything, and learn through simple "what-if"
  experiments.

The system converts every strategy into a standard, validated strategy format
and backtests it on 1-minute Nifty 50 and India VIX data from 2015 to 2026,
with option premiums modelled from spot and VIX. It then generates a clear
performance report, with a plain-English summary and risk shown as prominently
as profit, and suggests data-backed improvements or better-performing
alternative strategies.

The project must be built using specification-driven development and deployed
on Vercel.

Detailed scope

PROJECT: Nifty 50 Options Strategy Builder, Backtester & Advisor
Course assignment (spec-driven development). Must be built spec-first and deployed on Vercel.

GOAL
A web app where traders build Nifty 50 option strategies with almost any parameters,
and beginners learn using explained templates. Both get a backtest report and
data-backed suggestions to improve the strategy or switch to a better alternative.

USERS
- Trader (primary): builds custom strategies with full control
- Beginner (secondary): uses explained templates, plain-English reports and
  what-if sliders to learn safely

DATA (provided, in /data)
- Nifty 50 spot: 1-minute OHLC, 9 Jan 2015 – 15 May 2026
- India VIX: 1-minute, same period
- No option premium data: premiums must be MODELLED (see Pricing)

IN SCOPE (MVP)
1. Strategy builder (guided wizard), covering:
   - Timeframe (1/3/5/15/30/60-min, daily), resampled from 1-min data
   - Indicator library with user parameters, multiple instances allowed
     (Supertrend, EMA, SMA, RSI, ADX, MACD, VWAP, Bollinger Bands)
   - Entry conditions as [left][operator][right] rows with nested AND/OR groups;
     operators: >, <, >=, <=, crosses above/below, turns (state flip),
     slope up/down over N candles; separate CE and PE condition sets
   - Instrument: BUY/SELL, CE/PE, strike by ATM ± points / delta / premium,
     expiry (current weekly, next weekly, monthly); 1–4 legs
   - Exits: SL (trigger-candle high/low on premium, fixed points, %), SL cap,
     target (R:R or fixed), trail to cost at xR, exit after N candles, hard exit time
   - Position sizing: base lots + VIX-band multipliers
   - Session filters: entry windows, no-entry windows, expiry-day cutoff time
   - Daily limits: max trades/day, stop after N SL hits, max daily loss
2. Canonical strategy format: one JSON Schema = single source of truth,
   validated in both UI (TypeScript) and engine (Python/pydantic)
3. Clarification step: flag vague or missing values; no backtest until the
   strategy fully validates
4. Beginner mode:
   - Template gallery; each template has a one-paragraph plain-English
     explanation and a rules table ("Rule / Setting / What it means")
   - First template: "Simple Trend Follower" (15-min, 9/21 EMA crossover,
     buy ATM CE/PE, SL 30% of premium, target 50%, entries 09:30–14:30,
     force exit 15:15, max 2 trades/day, 1 lot)
   - Further templates: "Higher-Timeframe Trend" (1-hour version) and a simple
     short straddle, to show how option selling behaves differently
   - Run any template in one click, no building required; "Customize" opens
     it in the full builder
   - Tooltip on every field and a glossary (VIX, ATM/ITM/OTM, premium, SL, etc.)
   - What-if sliders (e.g., stop-loss %, target %) that re-run the backtest and
     show how results change
5. Backtest engine with:
   - Configurable slippage, brokerage and taxes
   - Dated expiry calendar and lot-size tables (never hardcode a weekday)
6. Report:
   - Equity curve, win rate, max drawdown, expectancy, worst losing streak
   - Breakdowns by year, VIX band, weekday/expiry day, CE vs PE
   - Plain-English summary for every report (e.g., "made money in 4 of 7 years;
     a ₹1 lakh account would have fallen to ₹62,000 at its worst point")
   - Risk-first layout: drawdown and losing streaks shown as prominently as profit
7. Suggestions engine:
   - Parameter sweeps
   - Loss-regime filters (e.g., exclude VIX < 12)
   - Comparison with standard alternative strategies
   Suggestions are edited strategy JSON, re-run and shown side by side as versions,
   each with a plain-English reason
8. Overfitting guard:
   - Weekly strategies: tune on 2019–2023, report on Jan 2024 – May 2026
   - Monthly strategies may use data from 2015

PRICING (modelled premiums)
- Black-Scholes per minute: spot, strike, India VIX as IV, minutes to expiry,
  fixed risk-free rate (configurable)
- Pricing behind a PricingProvider interface: BlackScholesVIXProvider now,
  RealOptionDataProvider possible later
- Every report shows a "Premiums modelled, not actual" badge plus a VIX ± 2
  sensitivity rerun

DATA RULES
- Weekly Nifty options start in 2019; earlier years are monthly-only
- Nifty weekly expiry was Thursday until contracts expiring from 1 Sep 2025, then Tuesday
- Load-time checks: missing minutes, holidays/special sessions, Nifty–VIX
  timestamp alignment, outlier spikes; log and skip bad days, never crash
- Convert CSV to Parquet at build time

ARCHITECTURE & DEPLOYMENT
- Next.js (React, TypeScript) frontend + Python serverless functions (pandas/numpy),
  one repo, deployed to Vercel Hobby via GitHub
- Each backtest/variant is a separate API call (stay well under the 300s limit);
  suggestion variants and what-if reruns run in parallel from the browser,
  max ~30 per sweep
- Storage: browser storage + JSON export/import (no database in MVP)

OUT OF SCOPE (MVP)
Live trading or broker integration, real option-chain data, user accounts,
adjustments/rolling of positions, instruments other than Nifty 50.
Optional bonus: plain-English/PDF → strategy JSON via an LLM.

BUILD ORDER (finish each end to end before adding more)
1. Spec docs + JSON Schema
2. Data loader and checks
3. Backtest engine + tests, using the Simple Trend Follower template first
4. Report (including plain-English summary)
5. Template gallery + one-click run (beginner path)
6. Full strategy builder (trader path)
7. Suggestions + what-if sliders
8. Deploy + README

SUCCESS CRITERIA
- Public Vercel URL; a beginner can run a template and read the summary, and a
  trader can build, validate, backtest and improve a strategy end to end
- Spec docs: requirements.md (user stories for both user types + Given/When/Then
  acceptance criteria), design.md, tasks.md, strategy.schema.json
- Automated tests mapped to acceptance criteria
- README with setup, assumptions, limitations and a disclaimer
  (backtests are not a guarantee of future returns)

HOW TO WORK
Do NOT write application code yet. First ask me clarifying questions, then produce
requirements.md, design.md, tasks.md and strategy.schema.json for my review.
The schema must express both the beginner "Simple Trend Follower" template and my
advanced sample strategy (15-min Supertrend/EMA/RSI/ADX ITM option buying).

One change worth noting: the build order now starts with the beginner template. It's the simplest strategy, so it's the fastest way to get one complete build → backtest → report path working before you tackle the full builder.

PROMPT 2026-10-13 23:41 UTC

In my case user can upload a doc with strategy and we will ask questions if data Missing to create json. Am I right?

Claude's response

Yes, exactly. That's a good feature, and it fits spec-driven development: the system turns an informal strategy into a precise spec before anything runs.

The flow

  1. Upload: the user uploads a PDF, Word file or text (like your Supertrend strategy).
  2. Extract: an LLM reads it and fills the strategy JSON. Each field is marked as:
    – Found: clearly stated ("RSI 14, above 55")
    – Vague: stated but not precise ("closes decisively above," "SL 30–35")
    – Missing: required but absent (e.g., expiry choice, number of lots)
    – Unsupported: a rule your engine can't run yet, flagged rather than silently dropped
  3. Ask: the system asks only about vague and missing fields, as simple choices where possible. For example: "SL cap: 30 or 35 points?"
  4. Confirm: it shows the final rules as a plain table, and the user approves before the backtest runs.
  5. Save: the result is validated JSON that the user can still edit in the builder.

One distinction from existing tools: [another product] already turns plain-English text into conditions. What I didn't see is document upload plus a clarification step that refuses to guess, so make that the explicit selling point: "the system never guesses your rules; it asks."

For your spec, write an acceptance criterion like: "Given an uploaded strategy with a vague rule, when it is parsed, then the system asks a clarifying question and does not run a backtest until it is answered."

My pick is options-strategy-lab. It's descriptive for graders and still fits once you add Bank Nifty, Sensex or MCX crude. If you might add futures too, derivlab is broader.

You can describe the MVP in the README as: "Options Strategy Lab, currently supporting Nifty 50."

One design point for the spec now: make the underlying a configurable instrument profile, not hardcoded Nifty logic. Each profile would hold:

  • Exchange and trading hours (MCX runs into the evening, unlike NSE)
  • Strike interval and lot-size table
  • Expiry calendar (e.g., Sensex expires on a different weekday than Nifty)
  • Volatility source (India VIX for Nifty; something else for crude)

That way, adding a new instrument later means adding a data file and a profile, not rewriting the engine. It's also a nice extensibility point to mention in your design doc.

PROMPT 2026-10-14 00:00 UTC

First you update the scope and problem statement for the other indexes and mcx addition thing. Then we will come back on name

Claude's response

Here are both, updated so the MVP stays Nifty 50 but the design is ready for other indices and MCX. I've used [PROJECT NAME] as a placeholder until you decide.

Problem statement

PROBLEM STATEMENT

Retail traders in Indian derivatives (index options such as Nifty 50, Bank Nifty
and Sensex, and commodity options on MCX such as crude oil) often trade
strategies built on indicators, rules and gut feel, without knowing how those
strategies would have performed historically. Their strategies usually exist as
informal notes or documents with vague rules ("exit if it closes decisively
below", "reduce size significantly"), which cannot be tested as written.
Existing tools can run backtests, but they mostly report how a strategy
performed, not why it worked or failed, whether the results are overfit, or how
to improve it. Beginners face an even bigger gap: they don't understand the
terminology and have no safe way to learn why most strategies fail.

Build a web application that serves two kinds of users:
- Experienced traders either upload their strategy as a document or build it
  in a guided builder using almost any parameters (timeframe, indicators, entry
  conditions, strike selection, stop-loss, target, trailing, time filters,
  volatility-based sizing, daily limits). For uploaded documents, the system
  extracts the rules and asks clarifying questions for anything vague or
  missing. It never guesses.
- Beginners start from ready-made templates explained in plain language, run
  them without building anything, and learn through simple "what-if"
  experiments.

Every strategy is converted into a standard, validated strategy format and
backtested on historical data, with option premiums modelled from the
underlying price and a volatility source. The system then generates a clear
performance report, with a plain-English summary and risk shown as prominently
as profit. It explains when and why the strategy works or fails, checks for
overfitting on unseen data, and suggests data-backed improvements or
better-performing alternatives.

The first release supports Nifty 50 options, using 1-minute Nifty 50 and India
VIX data from 2015 to 2026. The system must be designed so that other
instruments (Bank Nifty, Sensex, MCX crude oil and other commodities) can be
added later by supplying their data and an instrument configuration, without
rewriting the core engine.

The project must be built using specification-driven development and deployed
on Vercel.

Detailed scope

PROJECT: [PROJECT NAME]: Options Strategy Builder, Backtester & Advisor
Course assignment (spec-driven development). Must be built spec-first and deployed on Vercel.

GOAL
Turn any options strategy (an uploaded document, a guided build, or a template)
into a precise, validated spec; backtest it; and explain why it works or fails,
whether it is overfit, and how to improve it.
MVP instrument: Nifty 50 options. Architecture: instrument-agnostic, so Bank Nifty,
Sensex, MCX crude oil and others can be added later through configuration and data.

POSITIONING
Existing tools (e.g., [another product], [another product], [another product], [another product]) already offer strategy
builders and backtests with real option data. This project differentiates on:
- Document upload + clarification: the system never guesses rules; it asks
- Improvement suggestions with plain-English reasons, compared side by side
- Out-of-sample testing to expose overfitting
- Regime analysis: when the strategy works and fails (volatility bands, trend vs sideways)
- Beginner learning mode with risk-first, plain-English reports
- One consistent workflow across index and commodity options (future releases)
Pitch: "Existing tools tell you how a strategy performed. This one explains why,
warns when results are overfit, and suggests how to improve it."

USERS
- Trader (primary): uploads or builds custom strategies with full control
- Beginner (secondary): uses explained templates, plain-English reports and
  what-if sliders to learn safely

DATA
MVP (provided, in /data):
- Nifty 50 spot: 1-minute OHLC, 9 Jan 2015 – 15 May 2026
- India VIX: 1-minute, same period
- No option premium data: premiums must be MODELLED (see Pricing)
Future: each new instrument adds its own data files (underlying price, and a
volatility series where one exists) in the same normalized format.

INSTRUMENT PROFILES (multi-instrument design, required in MVP)
No instrument-specific logic is hardcoded in the engine. Each instrument is a
configuration file (e.g., /instruments/nifty50.json) defining:
- Identity: symbol, display name, exchange (NSE / BSE / MCX), segment
  (index options / commodity options)
- Trading session: market hours, including evening sessions for MCX
- Contract rules: strike interval, dated lot-size table, contract multiplier,
  tick size
- Expiry calendar: dated table of expiry types (weekly / monthly) and expiry
  days, including historical changes
- Underlying type: spot index, or futures contract (MCX options are on futures)
- Volatility source: e.g., India VIX for Nifty; historical volatility computed
  from price where no volatility index exists
- Pricing model: which PricingProvider applies (e.g., Black-Scholes for index
  options, Black-76 for options on futures)
- Costs: default slippage, brokerage and taxes for that exchange
- Data files: paths to the price and volatility series
The strategy JSON references an instrument by symbol ("underlying": "NIFTY50").
The builder, validation, backtest, report and suggestions read all
instrument-specific values from the profile.
MVP ships with exactly one profile: NIFTY50. A test must prove that adding a
second dummy profile requires no engine code changes.

IN SCOPE (MVP)
1. Strategy document upload (PDF, DOCX, TXT) with clarification:
   - An LLM extracts rules into the strategy JSON
   - Each field is marked: Found / Vague / Missing / Unsupported
   - The system asks targeted questions only for Vague and Missing fields,
     as multiple-choice where possible (e.g., "SL cap: 30 or 35 points?")
   - Unsupported rules are listed explicitly, never silently dropped
   - The user confirms a plain-English rules table before any backtest
   - The result opens in the builder for further editing
   - LLM API key stored as a Vercel environment variable
2. Strategy builder (guided wizard), covering:
   - Instrument selection (only profiles that exist; Nifty 50 in MVP)
   - Timeframe (1/3/5/15/30/60-min, daily), resampled from 1-min data
   - Indicator library with user parameters, multiple instances allowed
     (Supertrend, EMA, SMA, RSI, ADX, MACD, VWAP, Bollinger Bands)
   - Entry conditions as [left][operator][right] rows with nested AND/OR groups;
     operators: >, <, >=, <=, crosses above/below, turns (state flip),
     slope up/down over N candles; separate CE and PE condition sets
   - Instrument leg: BUY/SELL, CE/PE, strike by ATM ± N strikes or points /
     delta / premium, expiry (as allowed by the instrument's expiry calendar);
     1–4 legs
   - Exits: SL (trigger-candle high/low on premium, fixed points, %), SL cap,
     target (R:R or fixed), trail to cost at xR, exit after N candles, hard exit time
   - Position sizing: base lots + volatility-band multipliers
   - Session filters: entry windows, no-entry windows, expiry-day cutoff time
     (validated against the instrument's trading hours)
   - Daily limits: max trades/day, stop after N SL hits, max daily loss
3. Canonical strategy format: one JSON Schema = single source of truth,
   validated in both UI (TypeScript) and engine (Python/pydantic)
4. Validation gate: no backtest until the strategy fully validates against
   both the schema and its instrument profile, whichever way it was created
5. Beginner mode:
   - Template gallery; each template has a one-paragraph plain-English
     explanation and a rules table ("Rule / Setting / What it means")
   - First template: "Simple Trend Follower" (Nifty 50, 15-min, 9/21 EMA
     crossover, buy ATM CE/PE, SL 30% of premium, target 50%, entries
     09:30–14:30, force exit 15:15, max 2 trades/day, 1 lot)
   - Further templates: "Higher-Timeframe Trend" (1-hour version) and a simple
     short straddle, to show how option selling behaves differently
   - Run any template in one click; "Customize" opens it in the full builder
   - Tooltip on every field and a glossary (VIX, ATM/ITM/OTM, premium, SL, etc.)
   - What-if sliders (e.g., stop-loss %, target %) that re-run the backtest
6. Backtest engine with:
   - Costs, expiry calendar, lot sizes and session hours taken from the
     instrument profile (never hardcoded)
7. Report:
   - Equity curve, win rate, max drawdown, expectancy, worst losing streak
   - Breakdowns by year, volatility band, trend vs sideways periods,
     weekday/expiry day, CE vs PE
   - Plain-English summary explaining when and why the strategy works or fails
   - Risk-first layout: drawdown and losing streaks shown as prominently as profit
8. Suggestions engine:
   - Parameter sweeps
   - Loss-regime filters (e.g., exclude VIX < 12)
   - Comparison with standard alternative strategies
   Suggestions are edited strategy JSON, re-run and shown side by side as versions,
   each with a plain-English reason
9. Overfitting guard:
   - Nifty weekly strategies: tune on 2019–2023, report on Jan 2024 – May 2026
   - Nifty monthly strategies may use data from 2015
   - Report flags a strategy as "possibly overfit" when unseen-period results
     are much worse than the tuning period
   - Tuning and testing periods are configurable per instrument

PRICING (modelled premiums)
- Pricing behind a PricingProvider interface, selected by the instrument profile
- MVP: BlackScholesVIXProvider. Per minute: spot, strike, India VIX as IV,
  minutes to expiry, fixed risk-free rate (configurable)
- Future: Black76Provider (options on futures, e.g., MCX), HistoricalVolProvider
  (instruments without a volatility index), RealOptionDataProvider (actual
  premiums, if data becomes available)
- Every report shows a "Premiums modelled, not actual" badge plus a
  volatility ± 2 points sensitivity rerun

DATA RULES (Nifty 50 profile)
- Weekly Nifty options start in 2019; earlier years are monthly-only
- Nifty weekly expiry was Thursday until contracts expiring from 1 Sep 2025, then Tuesday
- Load-time checks (all instruments): missing minutes, holidays/special
  sessions, price–volatility timestamp alignment, outlier spikes; log and skip
  bad days, never crash
- Convert CSV to Parquet at build time

ARCHITECTURE & DEPLOYMENT
- Next.js (React, TypeScript) frontend + Python serverless functions (pandas/numpy),
  one repo, deployed to Vercel Hobby via GitHub
- Engine modules: data loader, instrument registry, indicator library,
  condition evaluator, pricing providers, backtest runner, report builder,
  suggestion engine; all instrument-agnostic
- Each backtest/variant is a separate API call (stay well under the 300s limit);
  suggestion variants and what-if reruns run in parallel from the browser,
  max ~30 per sweep
- Document parsing via an LLM API call from a serverless function
- Storage: browser storage + JSON export/import (no database in MVP)

OUT OF SCOPE (MVP)
- Instruments other than Nifty 50 (the design supports them; profiles and data
  are not shipped)
- Live trading or broker integration, real option-chain data, user accounts,
  adjustments/rolling of positions

FUTURE SCOPE (post-MVP)
- Add instrument profiles and data: Bank Nifty, Sensex, then MCX crude oil and
  other commodities
- Black-76 and historical-volatility pricing providers
- Cross-instrument comparison: run the same strategy on multiple instruments
- Real option-premium data where available

BUILD ORDER (finish each end to end before adding more)
1. Spec docs + JSON Schema + instrument profile schema
2. Instrument registry + NIFTY50 profile + data loader and checks
3. Backtest engine + tests, using the Simple Trend Follower template first
4. Report (including plain-English summary)
5. Template gallery + one-click run (beginner path)
6. Document upload + clarification flow, tested with my sample strategy PDF
7. Full strategy builder (trader path)
8. Suggestions + overfitting check + what-if sliders
9. Deploy + README

SUCCESS CRITERIA
- Public Vercel URL. End to end:
  - A beginner can run a template and read the plain-English summary
  - A trader can upload a strategy document, answer clarifying questions,
    and get a report
  - A trader can build, validate, backtest and improve a strategy in the builder
- Spec docs: requirements.md (user stories for both user types + Given/When/Then
  acceptance criteria), design.md, tasks.md, strategy.schema.json,
  instrument.schema.json, plus an "Existing solutions & differentiation" section
- Automated tests mapped to acceptance criteria, including:
  - "Given an uploaded strategy with a vague rule, when it is parsed, then the
    system asks a clarifying question and does not run a backtest until it is
    answered."
  - "Given a new instrument profile and data file, when it is registered, then
    strategies can be validated and backtested on it with no engine code changes."
- README with setup, assumptions, limitations, how to add a new instrument, and
  a disclaimer (backtests are not a guarantee of future returns)

HOW TO WORK
Do NOT write application code yet. First ask me clarifying questions, then produce
requirements.md, design.md, tasks.md, strategy.schema.json and
instrument.schema.json for my review. The strategy schema must express both the
beginner "Simple Trend Follower" template and my advanced sample strategy
(15-min Supertrend/EMA/RSI/ADX ITM option buying), which I will also share as a
PDF to test the upload flow.

The main changes are the Instrument Profiles section and the second acceptance test, which proves extensibility rather than just claiming it. For MCX, options are on futures, so they need the Black-76 pricing model and evening trading hours. That's why both are configuration items now, even though only Nifty ships in the MVP.

PROMPT 2026-10-14 00:08 UTC

For tech stake I have a change. I have AWS free acount as well and vercel account

Claude's response · 2 actions
  • Searched the web: AWS free tier 2026 new account free plan credits Lambda always free 1 million requests
  • Searched the web: AWS Lambda quotas max timeout 15 minutes memory 10240 MB container image size

Having both is useful. I'd split the work: Vercel for the website, AWS Lambda for the heavy backtesting.

Recommended setup

PartWhereWhy
UI (Next.js)VercelEasy GitHub deploys, free Hobby plan
Backtest engine (Python)AWS Lambda (container image)Much more headroom than Vercel
Data (Parquet files)Baked into the Lambda container imageNo separate storage service needed
API endpointLambda Function URLSimpler than API Gateway

Why move the engine to AWS

  • Lambda can run for up to 15 minutes and use up to 10,240 MB of memory, versus 300 seconds and 2 GB on Vercel Hobby. Big parameter sweeps can run in one call.
  • Container images can be up to 10 GB, so your 11 years of data fits easily inside the image.
  • Lambda's always-free allowance is 1 million requests and 400,000 GB-seconds per month, regardless of plan. That's thousands of backtests a month for free.

Things to check before relying on AWS

  • When was your AWS account created? Accounts created on or after 15 July 2025 on the Free plan close automatically after 6 months or when credits run out. If graders might open your app after that, upgrade to the Paid plan (always-free usage still applies) or keep the Vercel-only version as a fallback.
  • New AWS accounts have reduced concurrency and memory quotas, which AWS raises automatically with usage. Parallel sweeps may be throttled at first.
  • New accounts don't get free API Gateway calls or S3 storage, only credits. That's why I suggest Function URLs and baking the data into the image.
  • Set a billing alert on day one.

Trade-off: this adds Docker and AWS setup work. If your deadline is tight, Vercel-only still works. The hybrid version is stronger technically and looks good in a portfolio.

Updated ARCHITECTURE section to paste into your scope:

ARCHITECTURE & DEPLOYMENT
- Frontend: Next.js (React, TypeScript) on Vercel, deployed via GitHub
- Backtest engine: Python (pandas/numpy) on AWS Lambda, packaged as a
  container image; Parquet data baked into the image
- API: Lambda Function URL (CORS restricted to the Vercel domain)
- Document parsing: LLM API call from a Vercel serverless function
- Engine modules: data loader, instrument registry, indicator library,
  condition evaluator, pricing providers, backtest runner, report builder,
  suggestion engine; all instrument-agnostic
- Each backtest/variant is a separate Lambda call; suggestion sweeps and
  what-if reruns run in parallel, with a concurrency cap
- Storage: browser storage + JSON export/import (no database in MVP)
- Cost guardrails: AWS billing alert; stay within Lambda always-free limits
- Infrastructure defined as code (e.g., AWS SAM) so deployment is repeatable

Sources:

  • AWS Lambda quotas
  • AWS Free Tier explained (Rackspace)
  • Cloud provider free tiers 2026
  • AWS Builder Center: getting started with Free Tier

PROMPT 2026-10-14 00:12 UTC

I have created after 15th July and i have 6 months free only. I am fine with that. Later on I will look for some other solution of required

Claude's response

That works. Six months is plenty for building and grading the assignment. A few things will make moving off AWS painless later:

  • Keep the engine portable. Package it as a standard Docker container with a thin Lambda handler around it. The same container can later run on Google Cloud Run, Azure, or a small VPS with only the handler swapped.
  • Keep everything in GitHub: code, infrastructure-as-code files, and the data conversion script. When the Free plan ends, AWS closes the account and deletes its data after 90 days, so nothing should exist only in AWS.
  • Note the end date. Your account closes 6 months after creation. Put a reminder about a month before, and make sure your grading is done well before then.
  • Watch the credits. The account also closes early if credits run out, so set a billing alert and avoid anything that burns credits (like idle EC2 or NAT gateways).

Add one line to the ARCHITECTURE section of your scope:

- Portability: the engine is a provider-agnostic Docker container with a thin
  AWS Lambda handler, so it can move to another host (e.g., Cloud Run) by
  swapping only the handler and deploy config

That also reads well in a design doc: it shows you planned for the free tier ending rather than being caught by it.

DATA
MVP (provided, in /data):
- Nifty 50 spot: 1-minute OHLC, 9 Jan 2015 – 15 May 2026
- India VIX: 1-minute, same period
- No option premium data: premiums must be MODELLED (see Pricing)
Future: each new instrument adds its own data files (underlying price, and a
volatility series where one exists) in the same normalized format.

INSTRUMENT PROFILES (multi-instrument design, required in MVP)
No instrument-specific logic is hardcoded in the engine. Each instrument is a
configuration file (e.g., /instruments/nifty50.json) defining:
- Identity: symbol, display name, exchange (NSE / BSE / MCX), segment
  (index options / commodity options)
- Trading session: market hours, including evening sessions for MCX
- Contract rules: strike interval, dated lot-size table, contract multiplier,
  tick size
- Expiry calendar: dated table of expiry types (weekly / monthly) and expiry
  days, including historical changes
- Underlying type: spot index, or futures contract (MCX options are on futures)
- Volatility source: e.g., India VIX for Nifty; historical volatility computed
  from price where no volatility index exists
- Pricing model: which PricingProvider applies (e.g., Black-Scholes for index
  options, Black-76 for options on futures)
- Costs: default slippage, brokerage and taxes for that exchange
- Data files: paths to the price and volatility series
The strategy JSON references an instrument by symbol ("underlying": "NIFTY50").
The builder, validation, backtest, report and suggestions read all
instrument-specific values from the profile.
MVP ships with exactly one profile: NIFTY50. A test must prove that adding a
second dummy profile requires no engine code changes.

IN SCOPE (MVP)
1. Strategy document upload (PDF, DOCX, TXT) with clarification:
   - An LLM extracts rules into the strategy JSON
   - Each field is marked: Found / Vague / Missing / Unsupported
   - The system asks targeted questions only for Vague and Missing fields,
     as multiple-choice where possible (e.g., "SL cap: 30 or 35 points?")
   - Unsupported rules are listed explicitly, never silently dropped
   - The user confirms a plain-English rules table before any backtest
   - The result opens in the builder for further editing
   - LLM API key stored as a Vercel environment variable
2. Strategy builder (guided wizard), covering:
   - Instrument selection (only profiles that exist; Nifty 50 in MVP)
   - Timeframe (1/3/5/15/30/60-min, daily), resampled from 1-min data
   - Indicator library with user parameters, multiple instances allowed
     (Supertrend, EMA, SMA, RSI, ADX, MACD, VWAP, Bollinger Bands)
   - Entry conditions as [left][operator][right] rows with nested AND/OR groups;
     operators: >, <, >=, <=, crosses above/below, turns (state flip),
     slope up/down over N candles; separate CE and PE condition sets
   - Instrument leg: BUY/SELL, CE/PE, strike by ATM ± N strikes or points /
     delta / premium, expiry (as allowed by the instrument's expiry calendar);
     1–4 legs
   - Exits: SL (trigger-candle high/low on premium, fixed points, %), SL cap,
     target (R:R or fixed), trail to cost at xR, exit after N candles, hard exit time
   - Position sizing: base lots + volatility-band multipliers
   - Session filters: entry windows, no-entry windows, expiry-day cutoff time
     (validated against the instrument's trading hours)
   - Daily limits: max trades/day, stop after N SL hits, max daily loss
3. Canonical strategy format: one JSON Schema = single source of truth,
   validated in both UI (TypeScript) and engine (Python/pydantic)
4. Validation gate: no backtest until the strategy fully validates against
   both the schema and its instrument profile, whichever way it was created
5. Beginner mode:
   - Template gallery; each template has a one-paragraph plain-English
     explanation and a rules table ("Rule / Setting / What it means")
   - First template: "Simple Trend Follower" (Nifty 50, 15-min, 9/21 EMA
     crossover, buy ATM CE/PE, SL 30% of premium, target 50%, entries
     09:30–14:30, force exit 15:15, max 2 trades/day, 1 lot)
   - Further templates: "Higher-Timeframe Trend" (1-hour version) and a simple
     short straddle, to show how option selling behaves differently
   - Run any template in one click; "Customize" opens it in the full builder
   - Tooltip on every field and a glossary (VIX, ATM/ITM/OTM, premium, SL, etc.)
   - What-if sliders (e.g., stop-loss %, target %) that re-run the backtest
6. Backtest engine with:
   - Costs, expiry calendar, lot sizes and session hours taken from the
     instrument profile (never hardcoded)
7. Report:
   - Equity curve, win rate, max drawdown, expectancy, worst losing streak
   - Breakdowns by year, volatility band, trend vs sideways periods,
     weekday/expiry day, CE vs PE
   - Plain-English summary explaining when and why the strategy works or fails
   - Risk-first layout: drawdown and losing streaks shown as prominently as profit
8. Suggestions engine:
   - Parameter sweeps
   - Loss-regime filters (e.g., exclude VIX < 12)
   - Comparison with standard alternative strategies
   Suggestions are edited strategy JSON, re-run and shown side by side as versions,
   each with a plain-English reason
9. Overfitting guard:
   - Nifty weekly strategies: tune on 2019–2023, report on Jan 2024 – May 2026
   - Nifty monthly strategies may use data from 2015
   - Report flags a strategy as "possibly overfit" when unseen-period results
     are much worse than the tuning period
   - Tuning and testing periods are configurable per instrument

PRICING (modelled premiums)
- Pricing behind a PricingProvider interface, selected by the instrument profile
- MVP: BlackScholesVIXProvider. Per minute: spot, strike, India VIX as IV,
  minutes to expiry, fixed risk-free rate (configurable)
- Future: Black76Provider (options on futures, e.g., MCX), HistoricalVolProvider
  (instruments without a volatility index), RealOptionDataProvider (actual
  premiums, if data becomes available)
- Every report shows a "Premiums modelled, not actual" badge plus a
  volatility ± 2 points sensitivity rerun

DATA RULES (Nifty 50 profile)
- Weekly Nifty options start in 2019; earlier years are monthly-only
- Nifty weekly expiry was Thursday until contracts expiring from 1 Sep 2025, then Tuesday
- Load-time checks (all instruments): missing minutes, holidays/special
  sessions, price–volatility timestamp alignment, outlier spikes; log and skip
  bad days, never crash
- Convert CSV to Parquet with a script kept in the repo

ARCHITECTURE & DEPLOYMENT
- Frontend: Next.js (React, TypeScript) on Vercel, deployed via GitHub
- Backtest engine: Python (pandas/numpy) on AWS Lambda, packaged as a
  container image; Parquet data baked into the image
- API: Lambda Function URL (CORS restricted to the Vercel domain)
- Document parsing: LLM API call from a Vercel serverless function
- Engine modules: data loader, instrument registry, indicator library,
  condition evaluator, pricing providers, backtest runner, report builder,
  suggestion engine; all instrument-agnostic
- Each backtest/variant is a separate Lambda call; suggestion sweeps and
  what-if reruns run in parallel, with a concurrency cap
- Storage: browser storage + JSON export/import (no database in MVP)
- Infrastructure as code (e.g., AWS SAM) so deployment is repeatable
- Portability: the engine is a provider-agnostic Docker container with a thin
  AWS Lambda handler, so it can move to another host (e.g., Cloud Run) by
  swapping only the handler and deploy config
- Everything (code, infra config, data conversion script) lives in GitHub;
  nothing exists only in AWS

HOSTING CONSTRAINTS
- AWS account is on the Free plan: it closes 6 months after creation or when
  credits run out, whichever comes first
- Stay within Lambda's always-free allowance; avoid API Gateway, idle EC2 and
  NAT gateways; set an AWS billing alert on day one
- New AWS accounts start with reduced Lambda concurrency and memory quotas;
  the parallel sweep design must cope with throttling (retry with backoff)

OUT OF SCOPE (MVP)
- Instruments other than Nifty 50 (the design supports them; profiles and data
  are not shipped)
- Live trading or broker integration, real option-chain data, user accounts,
  adjustments/rolling of positions

FUTURE SCOPE (post-MVP)
- Add instrument profiles and data: Bank Nifty, Sensex, then MCX crude oil and
  other commodities
- Black-76 and historical-volatility pricing providers
- Cross-instrument comparison: run the same strategy on multiple instruments
- Real option-premium data where available
- Move the engine to a long-term host before the AWS Free plan ends

BUILD ORDER (finish each end to end before adding more)
1. Spec docs + JSON Schemas (strategy, instrument)
2. Instrument registry + NIFTY50 profile + data loader and checks
3. Backtest engine + tests (run locally first), using the Simple Trend
   Follower template
4. Report (including plain-English summary)
5. Deploy the engine to AWS Lambda + a minimal Vercel page that calls it
   (prove the full deployment path early)
6. Template gallery + one-click run (beginner path)
7. Document upload + clarification flow, tested with my sample strategy PDF
8. Full strategy builder (trader path)
9. Suggestions + overfitting check + what-if sliders
10. README + final deploy

SUCCESS CRITERIA
- Public Vercel URL backed by the AWS Lambda engine. End to end:
  - A beginner can run a template and read the plain-English summary
  - A trader can upload a strategy document, answer clarifying questions,
    and get a report
  - A trader can build, validate, backtest and improve a strategy in the builder
- Spec docs: requirements.md (user stories for both user types + Given/When/Then
  acceptance criteria), design.md, tasks.md, strategy.schema.json,
  instrument.schema.json, plus an "Existing solutions & differentiation" section
- Automated tests mapped to acceptance criteria, including:
  - "Given an uploaded strategy with a vague rule, when it is parsed, then the
    system asks a clarifying question and does not run a backtest until it is
    answered."
  - "Given a new instrument profile and data file, when it is registered, then
    strategies can be validated and backtested on it with no engine code changes."
- README with: the Nikasha story, setup, local run, deployment steps (Vercel +
  AWS), assumptions, limitations, how to add a new instrument, and a disclaimer
  (backtests are not a guarantee of future returns)

HOW TO WORK
Do NOT write application code yet. First ask me clarifying questions, then produce
requirements.md, design.md, tasks.md, strategy.schema.json and
instrument.schema.json for my review. The strategy schema must express both the
beginner "Simple Trend Follower" template and my advanced sample strategy
(15-min Supertrend/EMA/RSI/ADX ITM option buying), which I will also share as a
PDF to test the upload flow.

The main changes are the new Hosting Constraints section, the AWS architecture and portability lines, and build step 5. That step deploys a minimal end-to-end version early, so you find any AWS or Vercel setup problems before building the bigger features.

PROMPT 2026-10-14 01:20 UTC

let me know your thoughts on these refinements

Claude's response

All four refinements are good. Two need a small adjustment to work in practice.

1. No defaults for advanced options: agreed, with one distinction

Yes, that's what I meant. Advanced features (trailing, SL cap, daily limits, VIX-based sizing, no-entry windows, expiry-day cutoff) should simply be off unless the user turns them on. No hidden values.

A few settings aren't features, though. The engine can't run without some value for:

  • Lots: P&L needs a size.
  • Costs (brokerage, taxes, slippage): zero would make results look better than reality.
  • Backtest period
  • Intraday or positional: if the user gives only a target, should the trade close at day end or be held to expiry?

I'd suggest making "intraday or positional" a mandatory question, and showing the other three upfront on the review screen as visible, editable evaluation settings (e.g., "1 lot · costs ₹X per trade · 2019–2026"), not as silent assumptions. Nothing is applied without the user seeing it.

2. Data source in the disclaimer: yes, with two checks

Stating "Nifty 50 1-minute data from Kaggle, 9 Jan 2015 – 15 May 2026" is honest and good practice. Before you publish:

  • Check the Kaggle dataset's licence. Some datasets restrict reuse or require attribution. Credit the dataset by name and link.
  • Is the India VIX data also from Kaggle? If it's from a different source, the disclaimer should name both.

Please send me the dataset name(s) and link(s), and I'll write the exact wording. I'll also add a line that the data hasn't been independently verified and may contain errors.

3. No data storage: achievable, but the wording must be precise

You can genuinely store nothing: process uploads in memory, never write them to disk or a database, don't log request bodies, and set short log retention on AWS. Two honest caveats belong in the disclaimer, though:

  • Uploaded strategy text is sent to a third-party AI service for reading. That provider may keep it temporarily under its own policy. Saying "we store nothing" without this would be misleading.
  • The disclaimer acceptance has to be remembered somewhere. Options: keep it only in the user's browser (on their device, not your servers), or ask on every visit. I'd do the first and say so.

So the wording becomes: "Nikasha does not store your strategies, uploaded files, results or any personal data on its servers. Uploaded text is processed only to read your strategy and is sent to a third-party AI service for that purpose."

4. Waiting experience and download: good, with one consequence

With no storage and no accounts, there's nowhere to "come back" to. The result exists only in the open browser tab. So the design should be:

  • While evaluating: a progress screen with stage messages ("Preparing data… Simulating trades… Building your report…") and a rough time estimate.
  • The user can read educational content inside the app (glossary, "what is drawdown", "why most strategies fail") without leaving the tab.
  • When ready: a browser notification and a tab title change ("✓ Report ready").
  • A clear warning: "Keep this tab open. Closing it will lose your result."
  • Download result: a PDF report (and optionally a CSV of trades) generated in the browser, so nothing touches your servers. The download includes the disclaimer and the settings used.

Dropping Save/Load also means a strategy can't be reopened later. That's fine for the MVP, and simpler.

Send me the Kaggle dataset details and confirm the two points (intraday/positional as mandatory, visible evaluation settings), and I'll update NIKASHA_BRIEF.md with all of this.

CC0 is the most permissive option: the uploader has waived their rights, so you can use, modify and redistribute the data, even commercially, without asking permission. Attribution isn't required, but crediting the source (as the disclaimer already does) is good practice. Updating the brief.

  • design/: separate files for architecture, frontend, engine, pricing and deployment.
