PROMPT 2026-10-14 02:04 UTC

what is the best practice for SDD to generate spec? what you are suggesting is correct? and one file shuld be there for project requirements completly? I think which Nikahsa brief already has. let me know your thoughts

Claude's response · 4 actions
  • Searched the web: spec-driven development best practices spec kit constitution spec.md plan.md tasks.md
  • Searched the web: Kiro specs requirements.md design.md tasks.md EARS notation per feature
  • Read a web page: https://codemyspec.com/blog/github-spec-kit-guide
  • Read a web page: https://kiro.dev/docs/specs/feature-specs/requirements-first/

Your instinct is right, and my structure was close but not quite standard. Here's what current SDD practice looks like, and what I'd change.

What the established SDD tools do

The two most common workflows agree on a pattern:

  • GitHub Spec Kit: one project-wide constitution.md holds principles and governance. Then each feature gets its own folder under specs/, containing spec.md, plan.md, tasks.md, plus optional data-model.md, research.md and contracts/. Its workflow also has quality gates: a clarify step for underspecified areas and an analyze step that checks consistency across documents.
  • Kiro: each feature spec has three files: requirements.md (user stories, acceptance criteria and edge cases), design.md (architecture, data models, interfaces, error handling, testing strategy) and tasks.md. Requirements use EARS notation, "WHEN … THE SYSTEM SHALL …", because it's unambiguous and converts directly into tests.

So, two corrections to my suggestion

  1. Split by feature first, then by type. I grouped all requirements in one folder and all design in another. Standard practice is the reverse: one folder per feature containing its own requirements, design and tasks. Each feature then moves through spec → design → tasks → build on its own, which matches your build order.
  2. Yes, you need one complete project-level document, and you're right that the brief covers most of it. But the brief is a planning handoff: it mixes decisions, legal text, test data and instructions. I'd turn it into two clean living documents:
    – constitution.md: the non-negotiable rules every feature must obey: never guess rules, no defaults for advanced options, users never see JSON, zero data storage, no other product names, always disclose modelled premiums, never fill missing data.
    – product.md: the complete project requirements: problem, users, scope, data, non-functional requirements, glossary.

The brief stays in the repo as the source those were derived from.

Recommended structure

specs/
├── constitution.md        Non-negotiable principles
├── product.md             Complete project requirements (one file)
├── architecture.md        Shared: system design, Vercel + Lambda, data
├── contracts/             Shared: API contract, strategy + instrument schemas
├── traceability.md        Requirement ID → task → test
└── features/              One folder per feature, in build order
    ├── 01-data-and-engine/      requirements.md · design.md · tasks.md
    ├── 02-evaluation-and-report/
    ├── 03-strategy-builder/
    ├── 04-document-upload/
    ├── 05-beginner-mode/
    ├── 06-suggestions/
    └── 07-privacy-and-legal/

Write requirements in EARS ("WHEN the user selects ITM, THE SYSTEM SHALL ask for the distance in points"), with Given/When/Then examples for tests. Add the clarify and consistency-check gates before each feature's tasks are approved.

This is cleaner than what I put in the brief, and graders familiar with SDD tools will recognise it. Want me to update §7 of NIKASHA_BRIEF.md with this structure?

Sources:

  • GitHub Spec Kit guide (2026)
  • Kiro: requirements-first feature specs
  • Spec-driven development with spec-kit (Fred Hutch)

PROMPT 2026-10-14 02:07 UTC

and if i need to change anything first I need to uodate the spec then only we should aks code change right?

Claude's response

Yes, that's the core rule of spec-driven development: the spec is the source of truth, and code follows it. Any change in behaviour goes through the spec first.

The change flow

  1. Describe the change.
  2. Update the requirement in the relevant spec (new or changed EARS statement and acceptance criteria), and note it in a changelog.
  3. Update the design and contracts if they're affected.
  4. Run a consistency check so nothing else in the specs now contradicts it.
  5. Update or add tasks and tests.
  6. Only then change the code.

Sensible exceptions (no spec change needed):

  • Bug fixes where the code doesn't do what the spec already says. Fix the code and add a test for that requirement, but don't change the spec.
  • Refactoring that doesn't change behaviour.
  • Typos and styling.

A simple test: if the change alters what the user sees or what the system does, the spec changes first.

Make it enforced, not just intended. The common weak point in SDD is that specs drive the first build, then quietly drift out of date as people change code directly. To prevent that, put the rule in two places:

  • constitution.md: "Behaviour changes require a spec update first."
  • CLAUDE.md: "Before changing code, check whether the change alters behaviour. If it does, propose the spec update first and wait for approval."

Claude Code reads CLAUDE.md every session, so it will stop and ask for the spec change even when you forget.

Want me to update NIKASHA_BRIEF.md now with both the new spec structure and this change rule?

PROMPT 2026-10-14 02:13 UTC

dob't we need FE spec and BE spec file speratly or will it cover in above spec format?

Claude's response · 7 actions

It's partly covered, but there's a gap worth closing. Each feature's design.md would describe its own frontend and backend parts, which handles feature-specific work. But some things belong to neither feature: the frontend's overall navigation, design system and shared components, or the backend's module map, error handling and logging rules. Without a home, those get scattered or repeated. So the standard answer is both: shared layer docs for cross-cutting concerns, plus FE/BE sections inside each feature. Updating the brief.

  • Edited NIKASHA_BRIEF.md (6 times)
  • Shared file: NIKASHA_BRIEF.md

NIKASHA_BRIEF.md now covers the frontend and backend at two levels:

Project level: two new shared specs

  • frontend.md: everything that applies across the whole website: navigation and screen map, design system, shared components, in-memory-only state, loading and error states, accessibility, mobile layout, UI tone, and browser-side PDF/CSV export.
  • backend.md: everything that applies across the whole engine: module map, Lambda handler and portable container, instrument registry, error model, performance budgets, the no-user-content logging rule, and the AI extraction service.

Feature level: inside each feature folder

  • Every design.md has fixed Frontend, Backend and Contract sections. A one-layer feature (like data-and-engine) marks the other section "Not applicable."
  • Every task is tagged [FE], [BE] or [CONTRACT], and contract tasks come first. Once a feature's API shape is agreed, the frontend and backend can be built and tested independently.

How the pieces fit: architecture.md shows how the parts connect, frontend.md and backend.md set the rules for each layer, contracts/ is the agreement between them, and each feature folder fills in the specifics. Nothing is written twice.

52
PROMPT 2026-10-14 02:49 UTC

is it mandatory to create a git repo for this project for deployment ?

Claude's response

Not strictly mandatory, but I'd strongly recommend it.

Deploying works without Git:

  • Vercel: the Vercel CLI can deploy straight from your computer. On Hobby, CLI uploads are limited to 100 MB of source files, which is fine since the data lives in the engine, not the website.
  • AWS Lambda: the SAM CLI builds and deploys from your computer too.

Why you should still use a Git repo:

  • It proves your spec-driven process. Your success criteria say spec changes are recorded before related code changes. Commit history is the timestamped evidence of that order. Without it, graders only see the final state.
  • Your AWS account will close. The Free plan ends after 6 months, and the brief says nothing should exist only in AWS. A repo is where everything lives safely.
  • Graders usually expect a repo link alongside the live URL. Check your course's submission rules.
  • Safety while building. If Claude Code makes a change you don't like, you can roll back easily.
  • Automatic deploys: with Vercel connected to GitHub, every push redeploys the site.

Two practical notes:

  • Create the repo under your personal GitHub account, not a GitHub organization. Vercel's free Hobby plan can't connect to repositories owned by Git organizations.
  • It can be private if you prefer; Vercel still deploys it, and you can give graders access or make it public at submission.

So: create a nikasha repo on your personal GitHub, push the starter files (brief, data, samples), then open Claude Code in it.

Sources:

  • Vercel limits overview

PROMPT 2026-10-14 03:07 UTC

Claude's response · 5 actions
  • Ran a command: List project files and read the brief
  • Read NIKASHA_BRIEF.md
  • Ran a command: Show README and data/sample layout
  • Ran a command: Read data README, cleaning report, tracked files
  • Read nifty_weekly_options_strategy.pdf

I've read the brief, the data README and cleaning report, both sample strategies, and checked what's in git. I haven't written any code. Before I draft constitution.md and product.md, here are the points I need you to decide. Each has my recommendation in bold, so you can reply "all recommended except 4, 9" or similar.

### A. Repo things I found

  1. clean.py isn't in the repo. The brief and the data README both say it's kept for traceability, but only cleaning_report.json is here. Can you add it, or should the specs record it as missing?
  2. The two CSVs (44 MB and 55 MB) are committed to git directly. They're under GitHub's 100 MB per-file limit but over its 50 MB warning size. Keep them as they are; the Parquet files are generated at build time and gitignored. The other option is Git LFS.

### B. Course and project constraints

  1. Is there a deadline, and is this solo or a team? This decides how much of build steps 7–10 the specs plan in detail now and how much is kept short.
  2. LLM for document extraction (§6 open item): Anthropic API (Claude Sonnet 5.5), key in a Vercel env var. Do the course rules require or ban any provider? I'll quote that provider's data-retention policy in the privacy text once you confirm.
  3. Are the specs reviewed by someone else, like an instructor? If so, I'll write them for that reader.

### C. Engine behaviour (needs a fixed rule, which every report will disclose)

  1. Fill timing: the signal fires on candle close and the entry fills at the next minute's open premium. SL and target are checked on each 1-minute bar. If both are hit in the same minute, I assume SL was hit first (the conservative choice).
  2. Strike rounding: round spot to the nearest 50 for ATM, then apply the offset. Spot 24,523 → ATM 24,500 → 100 ITM CE = 24,400. The alternative is spot − 100, then round.
  3. One open position at a time. An opposite signal while a trade is open is ignored. Reversing the position would be an advanced option and stays OFF.
  4. Indicator warm-up: indicators carry over across days, the way charting tools do, and VWAP resets each day. Candles are anchored at 09:15, so on 1-hour the last candle is a short 15:15–15:29 bar.
  5. Positional trades held into expiry settle at intrinsic value using the expiry day's 15:29 close (Nifty options are cash-settled).
  6. Expiry calendar (§6): work it out from rules plus the trading days actually in the data. Weekly expiry is Thursday until Aug 2025 and Tuesday from 1 Sep 2025. Monthly expiry is the last such weekday of the month. If that day is a holiday, expiry moves to the previous trading day. Weekly options start 11 Feb 2019. This avoids needing an outside holiday list.
  7. Risk-free rate: 6.5% flat, set in the instrument profile and shown in Settings used.

### D. Report and evaluation settings

  1. Capital. Return % and drawdown % need a capital figure, and option selling needs margin, which we can't model. Report in ₹ only. Capital is an optional field; without it, % figures aren't shown.
  2. Costs (pre-filled and editable): ₹20 brokerage per order, current statutory STT, exchange, SEBI and stamp charges plus GST, and slippage of 0.5 premium points per side. Today's rates apply across the whole backtest, the same way lot size does. I'll check the exact current statutory rates when I write the specs.
  3. Default backtest period: the full range available for the chosen expiry type (weekly from Feb 2019, monthly from Jan 2015). The report always shows the in-sample and out-of-sample split separately.
  4. Trend vs sideways: daily ADX(14): 25 or above is trending, below 20 is sideways, and in between is "mixed".
  5. VIX bands: below 12, 12–18, 18–25, above 25.
  6. "Possibly overfit" flag: raised when out-of-sample expectancy per trade is below 50% of in-sample, or changes sign, with at least 30 trades in each period. Below that trade count, the report says "too few trades to judge" instead.

### E. Scope and templates

  1. The short straddle template needs two legs, a fixed entry time and selling. That brings multi-leg and short-side margin into the MVP engine. Keep it, but build it last among the templates. Or would you rather replace it with a single-leg template?
  2. The PDF's "reduce size significantly" for VIX above 18 has to be answered as a percentage, and sizing is rounded down to whole lots, with a minimum of 1. Is that OK? (Halving 1 lot would otherwise give 0.)
  3. UI language: English only for the MVP. The educational texts (drawdown, theta decay, and so on) are drafted by me and reviewed by you.

### F. Spec format

  1. Requirement IDs are fixed once approved: changed ones keep their ID and are noted in the CHANGELOG, and removed IDs are never reused. The specs live as Markdown in specs/.

Once you've answered, I'll write specs/constitution.md and specs/product.md and stop for your approval before going on to architecture and contracts.

I looked up when the Nifty expiry day changed, applied the rules to the trading days in your data, and recorded the result. Nifty expiry moved to Tuesday from 1 Sep 2025, as the spec already assumed. The research also confirmed the launch date of weekly options and showed that the April 2025 Monday plan never happened.

How Nifty expiry worked, 2015–2026:

Contracts expiringWeeklyMonthly
Before 11 Feb 2019none (weekly options didn't exist yet)last Thursday
11 Feb 2019 – 31 Aug 2025every Thursday (first: 14 Feb 2019)last Thursday
From 1 Sep 2025every Tuesday (first: 2 Sep 2025)last Tuesday
  • Monday plan: NSE announced a move to Monday expiry from 4 Apr 2025, then postponed it after a SEBI consultation and never brought it in. SEBI later required exchanges to use Tuesday or Thursday, and NSE chose Tuesday.
  • Holidays: if the expiry day is a holiday, expiry moves to the previous trading day.

Results on your data (9 Jan 2015 → 14 May 2026):

  • There are 136 monthly expiries, and 379 weekly or monthly expiries from Feb 2019.
  • The last Thursday expiry is 28 Aug 2025 and the first Tuesday expiry is 2 Sep 2025.
  • 21 expiries were moved earlier by holidays. I checked five of them against outside sources:
    – Diwali 2021: the 4 Nov holiday moved expiry to 3 Nov.
    – Bakri Id 2023: the NSE circular confirms expiry on Wed 28 June.
    – Diwali 2025: 21 Oct moved to 20 Oct.
    – Holi 2026: 3 Mar moved to 2 Mar.
    – Mahavir Jayanti 2026: 31 Mar moved to 30 Mar.
  • The other 16 come from the rules plus the days missing from the data.
  • Because trading days are read from the data, last-minute holiday changes are picked up automatically. The June 2023 Bakri Id holiday, which NSE moved at short notice, is one example.
  • Your rule that days removed during cleaning aren't holidays (the 2021-02-24 halt day) is in the spec, but it changes no date on the current data. That halt was on a Wednesday, and the Thursday after it traded normally.

I've updated specs/product.md so a trade is only entered when the whole strategy is satisfied, and logged the change in specs/CHANGELOG.md.

What "entire strategy satisfied" now means (§7.2). For a Call or Put entry, all six of these must be true at the same time. If any one fails, there is no trade:

  1. Every entry condition for that side is true on the closing candle.
  2. If a fixed entry time is set, it is that time.
  3. Every advanced rule the user turned on allows a new entry: entry and no-entry windows, the expiry-day cutoff, daily trade limit, stop after N stop-losses, and max daily loss.
  4. No position is already open, and for intraday it is before 15:15.
  5. The chosen expiry exists on that date (no weekly expiry before Feb 2019), and the entry minute has both Nifty and VIX data.
  6. The stop-loss and target can be worked out at entry, for example the trigger-candle low.

Other changes that follow from this:

  • Input 11 (entry trigger, §6.1): a strategy can now have indicator conditions and a fixed time together, and then both must hold. Before, it was one or the other.
  • Condition logic (§6.4): all listed conditions are joined by AND. OR is used only where the user's own rule says "or". If it isn't clear which they meant, the system asks.
  • Missed fills: if the minute after the signal has no price data, that entry is skipped. It is not taken late.
  • Report (§10): it now shows how many signals were skipped and why, for example "14 signals skipped: daily trade limit reached".

Please confirm one point. I kept OR available inside a rule, because the sample PDF needs it. Its ADX rule ("above 20, must slope up if between 20–30") can only be written as ADX > 30, or (ADX > 20 and rising). If you'd rather drop OR completely, that rule can't be represented, so it would be listed as unsupported.

PROMPT 2026-10-14 06:41 UTC

Can you tell me for what parts we need LLM? Please dont modifiy anything now. I need clear answer and purpose of LLM like where and for purpose it will use . also what we can accomplish without LLM?

Claude's response

Nikasha needs an LLM in one place only: reading a strategy that a user uploads or pastes as free text. Everything else is ordinary code, and it should stay that way.

## Where the LLM is used

Feature 04, document upload. When a trader uploads a PDF, DOCX or TXT file, or pastes text, a normal file-reading library turns the file into text. No LLM is involved in that step. The LLM is used only to understand the text:

StepWho does itPurpose
1. Read the file into textCode (file-parsing library)Get the words out of the PDF or DOCX
2. Understand the text and turn it into rulesLLMTurn free wording like "buy ITM 100 below spot when Supertrend turns green" into structured rules, quoting the sentence each rule came from
3. Ignore text that isn't a ruleLLMSkip lines like the "medical advice" footer without asking about them
4. Spot vague wordingLLMFlag phrases like "decisively", "30–35", "reduce significantly" and "Thursday"
5. Write the question for each vague ruleLLMSuggest the question and the choices, e.g. "SL cap: 30 or 35 points?"
6. Decide which mandatory inputs are missingCodeThe list of 13 mandatory inputs is fixed, so code checks it reliably
7. Ask for missing mandatory inputsCodeFixed questions, e.g. "Intraday or positional?"
8. Check the rules are valid and completeCodeValidate against the strategy format and the instrument profile
9. Show the rules in plain English for reviewCodeBuilt from the validated rules using fixed sentence patterns

That is about one LLM call per upload.

Guardrail: nothing the LLM returns is used directly. Its output is checked against the strategy format, every rule is shown to the user in plain English, and nothing runs until the user clicks Evaluate Strategy. If the LLM gets something wrong, the user sees it and can correct it.

## What works without an LLM

FeatureLLM needed?
Templates (beginner path)No
Guided strategy builderNo
Review & Evaluate screenNo
Backtest engine: data, indicators, option pricing, trades, costs, expiry calendarNo
Report: numbers, charts, breakdowns, overfitting checkNo
Plain-English summary in the reportNo. It's built from the computed numbers with fixed sentence patterns
Suggestions and what-if slidersNo. They re-run the engine with changed settings
Glossary, tooltips, educational contentNo. The text is written in advance
PDF/CSV download, disclaimer, privacyNo

So everything except reading free-text strategies works without an LLM. Without one:

  • What's lost: the document-upload path. A trader would have to enter the strategy in the guided builder instead. That path is one of the three success criteria ("a trader uploads a strategy document, answers clarifying questions and gets a report") and one of Nikasha's main selling points.
  • What still works: templates and the guided builder cover everything else.

## Where I'd deliberately not use an LLM

  • Report summary. An LLM could make the summary sound more natural, but it could also misstate a number or drift into advice-like wording, which conflicts with the "honesty" and "educational, not advisory" principles in the constitution. It would also send results to a third party, which weakens the "stores nothing" promise. Fixed sentence patterns built from the actual numbers are safer.
  • Anything that computes results. The LLM never calculates P&L, picks strikes or decides trades.

## What this means for privacy

The only user content that ever leaves Nikasha is uploaded strategy text, sent to Anthropic for steps 2–5. That's why the disclaimer mentions it and quotes Anthropic's 30-day retention policy.

I haven't changed any files. I'd keep the specs as they are, because they already limit the LLM to document upload. Approve constitution.md and product.md when you're ready, and I'll move on to architecture.

Decisions to check before approving:

  1. The browser calls the engine directly, not through Vercel. This avoids Vercel's time limits on long backtests. The catch is that the engine address is public and needs no login. That's limited by strict input checks, AWS concurrency limits and a billing alert, and nothing is stored there to steal.
  2. The AI service needs a simpler format. It can't return the nested condition format directly, so it returns a flat version and code converts it. A side effect: the AI is called once per upload, and the answers to clarifying questions are applied in the browser without calling it again.
  3. No per-user rate limit on uploads. Limiting per user would mean tracking users, which breaks "stores nothing". An Anthropic monthly spend limit controls cost instead.
  4. ITM/OTM distance must be in steps of 50. product.md allowed "must be a multiple or is rounded". I chose "must be a multiple", so an uploaded "75 points" gets asked about instead of silently rounded.
  5. The sample-PDF example uses made-up answers: "decisively" = 10 points, SL cap 30, VIX above 18 → 50% size. They only show that the format can hold the strategy; they're never used as defaults.
  6. Each backtest call also runs the VIX −2 and +2 versions internally. The speed target for all three together is 30 seconds.
  7. Library and hosting choices:
    – Charts with Recharts; PDF with jsPDF; validation with Ajv on the web and jsonschema in the engine, both reading the same schema files.
    – Light theme only for the MVP.
    – The engine is hosted in AWS Mumbai (ap-south-1), with 1-day log retention.

Still open, and where each will be decided:

  • VWAP: index data has no volume, so VWAP either gets a volume-free variant or is dropped (data-and-engine feature).
  • End of data: how a positional trade still open on the last data day is closed. I added an "end of data" exit reason as a placeholder (data-and-engine feature).
  • Short straddle: whether stop-loss and target apply per leg or to both legs combined (beginner-mode feature).
  • Timeframes: the exact list; the schema currently allows 1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 30 and 60 minutes (strategy-builder feature).

The deadline is 16 Oct. Once you approve, I'll write features/07-privacy-and-legal/ (requirements, then design, then tasks) and then the data-and-engine feature spec. That's the point where the build can start.

3. Short straddle uses one combined stop-loss. Both legs are treated as one position and exit together. The stop-loss (and target, if set) works on the combined premium. For example, selling CE ₹100 and PE ₹100 with a 30% stop exits both when the combined loss reaches ₹60 per unit. This applies to all multi-leg strategies. Multi-leg strategies can't use a trigger-candle stop-loss.

4. Timeframes are now 1, 3, 5, 15, 30, 60 minutes and 4 hours.

  • Please confirm 60 minutes. Your list didn't mention it, but the Higher-Timeframe Trend template runs on 1-hour candles. I kept it; say if you want it removed, and the template will need another timeframe.
  • 4-hour candles start at 09:15, so each day has two: 09:15–13:14 and a shorter 13:15–15:29. An intraday strategy on 4-hour candles can only act once a day, on the 13:14 close, because the second candle closes after the 15:15 square-off. The review screen will point this out.

I've made both changes, logged them in CHANGELOG.md, and the contract files still pass validation.

1. Positional trades: the system now asks.

  • If a positional strategy has no target and no time exit, the user is asked: "Your strategy has no target or time exit. What should happen to an open position?" The choices are:
    – Hold until expiry
    – Exit at a set time on expiry day (they enter the time)
    – Set a target
    – Exit after a number of candles

Nothing is pre-selected.

  • The "hold until expiry" option is back in the strategy format, and the check that a positional strategy has an exit (SEM-07) is reinstated.
  • What still happens without asking: if a trade with only a target reaches expiry day without hitting it, the contract expires and is settled at its intrinsic value. That's how options work, not a choice made for the user. The report will show the exit reason as "settled at expiry".
  • For the one-line sample, this question appears only if the user picks Positional. With Intraday, the 15:15 square-off is the exit and nothing more is asked.
  • Positional entries whose expiry falls after the data ends (14 May 2026) are still skipped and counted. That's because a trade the user chose to hold to expiry couldn't be completed with the data available.

2. Users are told why VWAP isn't available. The same note appears in three places:

  • the builder's indicator list, as a greyed-out VWAP entry with the note
  • the upload results, when a strategy mentions VWAP (marked as not supported)

PROMPT 2026-10-14 13:12 UTC

in architecture.md why we are keeping Anthropic API Claude Sonnet 5.5 with FE, I thinkl we should use python sdk for the same and use it via aws backend code. what is your thought. lets discuss first before doing any changes

Claude's response

The current design doesn't run the LLM call in the browser. /api/extract is a server-side Vercel function, and the API key stays on the server. But your proposal is better, and I recommend it with one adjustment: run extraction as a separate Lambda function, not inside the backtest engine.

## Why moving it to AWS and Python is better

  1. One backend in one language. All server logic would be Python on AWS. Vercel would only serve the web pages and never see user content. The privacy statement and the privacy audit then cover one platform instead of two, and the logging rules are written and tested once.
  2. No Vercel time limit. Extraction with Sonnet 5.5 can take 10–40 seconds, and Vercel's limit depends on the plan, which was an open risk. Lambda allows up to 15 minutes.
  3. Better document readers in Python. pypdf and python-docx are mature, and pydantic fits the Python SDK's structured outputs (client.messages.parse()) directly.
  4. Shared code. The Python code that converts the AI's draft into the strategy format can reuse the engine's validation models.

## The costs, and how to handle them

ConcernHandling
The API key moves to AWSStore it as an encrypted Lambda environment variable, not in Secrets Manager, which is a paid service. It's set at deploy time and never committed to the repo.
Memory cost. Extraction mostly waits on the AI. On the engine's 2 GB setting, each upload would cost about as much free-tier compute as a backtest.A separate small Lambda function (~512 MB, 120 s timeout) in the same deployment template, with its own address. It costs about a quarter as much and keeps the engine purely about backtesting.
Public endpoint that triggers paid AI callsSame risk as the Vercel route today. The Anthropic monthly spend limit stays the main control.
Upload size. Lambda receives file uploads base64-encoded, which adds about 33%, against a 6 MB limit.Keep the 4 MB file limit (about 5.4 MB once encoded). Same user-facing limit as now.
Concurrency. New AWS accounts may allow as few as 10 Lambda calls running at once.Backtests and extraction would share that limit. Fine at MVP scale.
More depends on AWS, and the AWS Free plan closes after 6 monthsExtraction gets the same portable structure as the engine (a thin handler around transport-free code), so both move together.
"Missing input" questionsThese would be generated in the browser by the same check the builder and review screen already use. Extraction returns only what the AI found plus the draft. This also means "missing" is decided in one place.

## What it changes in the specs, which also differ from the brief

  • Brief decisions to replace: "Document parsing: LLM call from a Vercel serverless function" and "LLM API key as a Vercel environment variable". I'd record that in CHANGELOG.md as your decision.
  • architecture.md:
    – Diagram: extraction becomes a second Lambda function, and Vercel serves static pages only.
    – The data-flow table: Vercel touches no user content.
    – Deployment: one Lambda function becomes two.
  • backend.md §9:
    – Rewritten for Python: the anthropic SDK, messages.parse() with a pydantic model, pypdf and python-docx.
    – Same model (claude-sonnet-5-5), same effort level, same refusal fallback.
  • frontend.md: the upload goes to the extraction function's address, and the security settings allow it.
  • api.openapi.yaml: /api/extract becomes /v1/extract on a third server entry, the extraction function. Missing-input questions move out of that response.
  • product.md: only the line about where the API key lives. The disclaimer is unchanged, because it names Anthropic, not Vercel.

My recommendation: move extraction to its own Python Lambda function, as described. Should I make these changes?

PROMPT 2026-10-14 13:26 UTC

and are you fine with this approache? as I dont want to sped $ for deployment now. After MVP UAT testing I will plan. Now let me you suggestion

Claude's response

Yes, I'm comfortable with it, and moving extraction to AWS doesn't add any cost. Everything except the AI calls fits inside free tiers.

## What each piece would cost

ItemCost for MVP and UAT
Vercel Hobby plan (web app)Free. Hobby is meant for personal, non-commercial projects, which fits an educational assignment.
AWS Lambda, both the engine and the extraction functionFree. The always-free allowance (1M requests and 400,000 GB-seconds a month) covers thousands of backtests. A second function adds nothing.
Lambda Function URLsFree. No API Gateway.
Container image storage (ECR)About ₹5–10 a month for a few hundred MB, covered by the AWS Free plan credits.
CloudWatch logs (1-day retention)Effectively free at this volume.
AWS Budgets alertFree.
Secrets ManagerNot used, so ₹0. The key goes in an encrypted Lambda environment variable.
Anthropic APIThe only real spend. Each upload costs a few US cents. Anthropic needs prepaid credits, and I believe the minimum purchase is about $5; check the console. That would cover hundreds of uploads.

AWS still needs a card on file to open the account, even on the Free plan. The budget alert from day one is there so nothing is charged by surprise.

## My suggestion: build and test locally, deploy only when needed

The architecture already allows everything to run on your laptop at no cost:

  • Web app: next dev on localhost.
  • Engine and extraction: the same Python code, run through the local HTTP adapter (or Docker). No AWS needed.
  • Tests: automated tests use recorded AI responses, so they never call Anthropic and cost nothing. Live AI calls happen only when you try an upload yourself.

So:

  1. Build and run UAT locally, at ₹0 except a few cents per live upload test.
  2. Deploy to Vercel Hobby and the AWS Free plan when you need the public URL. The brief's success criteria ask for "a public Vercel URL backed by the Lambda engine", so if your course submission on 16 Oct needs a live link, this step is still needed, still at ₹0.
  3. After UAT, decide on long-term hosting. The engine is portable, so that's a config change, not a rewrite.

The only cost decision for you is the Anthropic credit: do you already have one, for example through your course? Without it the upload path can't be tested live. Templates and the builder work fine without it.

## What I'd change, if you agree

  • Moving extraction to a separate Python Lambda function, as discussed. The spec changes are the ones I listed last time.
  • A new "Cost and local-first development" section in architecture.md. It would say:
    – the MVP runs fully on a local machine
    – automated tests never call paid services
    – deployment uses only free tiers
    – no paid AWS services (Secrets Manager, API Gateway, NAT gateways, EC2)
    – the Anthropic spend limit and the AWS budget alert are set before the first live call

Should I go ahead with both changes?

25
PROMPT 2026-10-14 16:18 UTC

one question. use can selcect multiple indicators and set the parameter for that right?

Claude's response

Yes. A user can add several indicators, set each one's parameters, and use the same indicator more than once with different settings.

How it works in the strategy format ([strategy.schema.json](specs/contracts/strategy.schema.json)):

  • Up to 20 indicators per strategy, each with its own settings. Your PDF sample uses five: Supertrend (10, 2), EMA 9, EMA 21, RSI 14 and ADX 14.
  • The same indicator can appear more than once, e.g. EMA 9 and EMA 21, or RSI 14 and RSI 7.
  • Each type has its own parameters, with allowed ranges:
IndicatorParameters
EMA / SMAPeriod (1–500), price source (open / high / low / close)
RSIPeriod (2–200)
ADXPeriod (2–200); also gives +DI and −DI
SupertrendPeriod (1–200), multiplier (up to 10); gives the line and its green/red direction
MACDFast, slow, signal periods; gives MACD line, signal line, histogram
Bollinger BandsPeriod (2–500), std. deviation (up to 5), price source; gives upper, middle, lower bands
  • Indicators can be combined in conditions, e.g. "EMA 9 crosses above EMA 21 AND RSI 14 > 55 AND Supertrend turns green". They can also be compared with price, India VIX or fixed numbers. Each side (Call/Put) allows up to 30 conditions, with AND/OR groups nested up to 4 levels.

Two limits worth knowing:

  1. One timeframe per strategy. All indicators use the strategy's candle timeframe. A rule like "15-min entry, but only if the 1-hour EMA is rising" (multi-timeframe) isn't supported in the MVP. An uploaded rule like that would be listed as Unsupported. Tell me if you want it; it would be a scope addition.
  2. Only the six indicators above. VWAP is excluded, with the reason shown to users, and custom indicators aren't supported.

Open for the builder spec (feature 03): whether parameter fields start empty or show common values (e.g. RSI 14) as suggestions the user must confirm. Following the "no hidden defaults" principle, I'd make common values visible suggestions that the user must confirm, never silently applied. I'll raise it at that feature's clarify step.

GroupIDsCovers
Disclaimer gate and acceptance01–09Open pages vs. gated pages, the checkbox, returning to the page the visitor asked for, nothing shown before acceptance, the browser flag, asking again if the text changes, blocked storage, waiting for the real dates

  4. Should "Continue" wait until the real data dates have loaded? The catch: if the engine is down, nobody can get past the disclaimer. Proposed: yes, wait, with a "Try again" button, because accepting a disclaimer with missing dates weakens it.

Design summary. Most of this feature is shared building blocks that the other features must reuse:

PartHow it works
Disclaimer gatePages are split into "open" (intro, disclaimer, learn) and "gated" (everything else). On a gated page the disclaimer appears in place, at the same address. After "I agree" the requested page appears, and nothing from it loads before then.
Acceptance memoryOne small module is the only code allowed to use browser storage, and a lint rule blocks storage and cookies everywhere else. The stored version is a fingerprint of the disclaimer text. New data dates don't ask users again; changed wording does.
Disclaimer textIt exists in one place. A test checks it word for word against product.md §11, so the app can't drift from the approved text. A script fills the README copy with the real dates from the data, and CI checks it stays current.
ComponentsFull disclaimer, short disclaimer, footer, data-source line, and the upload notice that names Anthropic with its 30-day retention. There are also two helpers that put the disclaimer into every PDF and CSV.
Browser hardeningSecurity headers allow connections only to the site and the two AWS functions. No console output in production builds, and no third-party scripts.
Python loggingA shared helper logs only allow-listed technical fields. For errors it records the error type and code location, never the error message, which could contain user values. Third-party library logs are silenced.
UploadsFiles are read entirely in memory and never touch disk. The file name is ignored. Only the text goes to Anthropic.
AWS setup1-day log retention. A CI test fails if any storage service ever appears in the deployment template.
ChecksOne shared banned-phrase list for the web app and the engine. Product-name check (see below). An end-to-end privacy test plants a marker string and confirms it never shows up in any log or the browser console, without any paid AI calls.

Two choices to check:

  1. Product-name check. The check against competitor names stores only scrambled fingerprints (SHA-256 hashes), not the names, because listing them in the repo would break the very rule it enforces. You keep the actual list privately. Is that OK?
  2. Repo layout. The design adds pylib/ (shared Python code) and scripts/ (repo checks) to the repo. I added them to architecture.md's repo layout; that changes no behaviour.

The design ends with a consistency check against the constitution, product.md, the architecture, frontend and backend specs, and the contracts. No contradictions were found.

    – data loading and quality checks

  • 34 acceptance tests built from concrete numbers in your examples. For instance:
    – 24,500 ITM 100 → 24,400 CE
    – entry 120 with trigger-candle low 80 and cap 30 → SL 90, target 165
    – 4 candles from a 10:15 entry → exit at 11:15
    – 2 lots × 65 → 130 on every trade
    – the ADX rule checked at 25 rising, 25 falling, 35 falling and 30 falling

Questions for you (my proposed answer follows each):

  1. Indicator formulas: use the standard textbook definitions most charting tools use (EMA seeded with a simple average; RSI, ATR and ADX with Wilder's smoothing; Supertrend on the bar's midpoint). Proposed: yes; the exact formulas go in the design.
  2. A positional signal on the day's last candle (closes 15:29): fill at the next day's 09:15? Proposed: no, skip it. The rules were met at yesterday's close, not after an overnight gap. This adds a new skip reason, "next-day fill", to the API.
  3. Trigger-candle stop-loss lands on the wrong side of the entry price (e.g. the price opens below the candle's low): Proposed: skip the trade. Falling back to the SL cap would be a rule you didn't write.
  4. Strategy trades both calls and puts, and both conditions are true on the same candle: Proposed: take neither and count it as "conflicting signals" (another new skip reason). Picking one would be a guess.
  5. Should "stop after 2 SL hits" also count exits at the trailed stop (break-even)? Proposed: no, only real stop-loss exits.
  6. Strike search by delta or premium: look within ±20 strikes (±1,000 points) of ATM? Proposed: yes.
  7. If data fails a quality check, drop the whole day or only the bad rows? Proposed: the whole day, because candles from a partly bad day can't be trusted. The current data passes every check, so this only matters for future data.

If 2 and 4 are approved, I'll add the two new skip reasons to api.openapi.yaml and log that change before writing the design.

Requirements and contract changes from your answers:

What the design fixes exactly. This is the most technical spec, so it pins down everything that could otherwise be read two ways:

AreaWhat's defined
Data in memoryNifty and VIX joined by minute. Missing values stay empty and are never filled. Each minute is flagged as priceable or not. Days that fail a quality check are dropped from both series. About 70 MB in memory.
Expiry calendarThe algorithm in pseudocode, including how monthly expiries also count as that week's expiry, and how dates after the data ends are handled.
CandlesBucketing from 09:15, never across days, built only from minutes that exist.
IndicatorsA table with the exact formula and warm-up length for each indicator. Test reference values come from hand calculations plus an independent open-source library, used only to generate test data.
ConditionsThe exact rule for each condition type. Any missing input makes the condition false. VIX shifts affect only prices, never signals.
PricingThe full Black-Scholes formulas, the minimum floors on volatility and time to expiry, rounding to 0.05, and the price range within a minute.
Signals and the 14 checksWhen a signal fires for each strategy shape (conditions only, fixed time only, or both). The 14 entry checks in order, with the first one that fails recorded as the skip reason.
Trade simulationA table of stop-loss and target levels for bought and sold options (percent, points, trigger candle, cap, risk-reward, trail to cost). The combined-premium rule for multi-leg strategies. The minute-by-minute exit search, including which exit wins on ties and how gaps are filled.
Sizing, costs, speedRounding for VIX sizing and when the "had no effect" note is added. Costs calculated on prices after slippage. Speed comes from vectorising per day and looping only over signals.

Two details in the design for you to check:

  1. The price range within a minute uses that minute's closing VIX value. It's a small modelling choice inside "Black-Scholes with India VIX", and it will be listed in the report's "How this was simulated" section.
  2. Combining a fixed entry time with conditions (e.g. "at 09:30, if RSI > 55"): the conditions are checked on the last candle that closed before 09:30.

The design ends with a consistency check against the constitution, product.md, the backend spec and the contracts. Apart from the contract change above, it found only small clarifications, and those are listed.

PROMPT 2026-10-14 17:01 UTC

what is the use of this file specs/features/01-data-and-engine/expiry-calendar-check.md

Claude's response

That file records the expiry-date check you asked for at the start. When we agreed how expiry dates would be worked out, you added two conditions: days removed during cleaning (like the 2021-02-24 halt day) must not shift expiries, and the derived dates must be spot-checked against known real expiries, with the check written down. This file is that written record.

It does three jobs:

1. The answer key for the engine's tests. The engine works out every expiry date itself, from the rules in the Nifty 50 profile plus the trading days in the data. This file holds the result that calculation must reproduce:

  • 136 monthly expiries
  • 379 weekly-or-monthly expiries from Feb 2019
  • the first weekly expiry (14 Feb 2019)
  • the last Thursday expiry (28 Aug 2025) and the first Tuesday expiry (2 Sep 2025)
  • all 21 expiries moved earlier by holidays

Requirement REQ-DAT-17 and test AC-DAT-08 say the engine's calendar must match this list exactly. If an engine change ever shifts one date, that test fails.

2. Evidence that the rules are right. The engine's calendar can't check itself. This file shows the rules were compared with outside sources:

  • Five holiday shifts confirmed against NSE circulars or official holiday lists, e.g. Bakri Id June 2023 (expiry moved to Wednesday 28 June) and Diwali 2025.
  • The history of expiry-day changes, with sources: weekly options launched 11 Feb 2019; the April 2025 Monday plan that never happened; the switch to Tuesday from 1 Sep 2025.
  • Which shifted dates were confirmed externally and which were derived only from the data.

3. A record of tricky decisions, so nobody undoes them later by mistake:

  • The halt day (2021-02-24) and the cut-off day (2026-05-15) count as trading days, so they never shift an expiry. On the current data this rule changes nothing, and the file says so.
  • Muhurat-only days (e.g. Diwali, 4 Nov 2021) are real holidays, so they do shift expiries.

So it's reference material, not a requirements or design file. That's why it sits next to requirements.md and design.md in the data-and-engine folder rather than being one of them. If NSE changes its expiry rules or new data is added, this file gets updated first, and then the engine is changed to match it.

The data-and-engine design is still waiting for your approval. After that I'll write its tasks.md.

What's in it:

  • 13 evaluation requirements:
    – Review screen: every rule in plain English with unused options shown as "Not used"; period, costs with sources, and optional capital; confirming pre-filled values; the Evaluate button stays disabled until nothing is missing and names what is; the 4-hour intraday note; the short disclaimer.

Questions for you (my proposed answer follows each):

  1. How does "edit any line" on the Review screen work? Proposed: each rule has an Edit link that opens the guided builder at that step, with the strategy loaded. This works for templates and uploads too. Settings are edited directly on the Review screen.
  2. How are pre-filled values confirmed? Proposed: lots and lot size each get a Confirm button. Costs and period get one checkbox, "I've checked the costs and period". Changing a value also counts as confirming it.
  3. Can a running evaluation be cancelled? Proposed: yes. Cancel goes back to Review with the strategy unchanged.
  4. Overfitting check when the strategy already loses money in 2019–2023: what should it say? Proposed: "not applicable", with the text "The strategy lost money in the earlier period too, so there is no good result to test for overfitting." Flagging overfitting only makes sense for a result that looked good.
  5. Do break-even trades (exactly ₹0) count as wins or losses? Proposed: neither. They still count toward total trades, so win rate = wins ÷ all trades. A break-even trade also ends a losing streak.
  6. How many trades must a group have before the summary calls it "best" or "worst" (e.g. "worked best when VIX was 12–18")? Proposed: at least 10. Smaller groups still appear in the tables, marked "few trades".
  7. Should the trades CSV appear even when there are no trades? Proposed: no. The button is hidden, and the PDF says "no trades".

Once you approve these, I'll write the design. That will include the report layout and chart design.

The evaluation-and-report requirements are approved with your seven answers, and the design is drafted: [features/02-evaluation-and-report/design.md](specs/features/02-evaluation-and-report/design.md).

One finding to check: chart colours. I ran the colour-blind check on the palette. The brand's green-for-profit and red-for-loss pair lands in the warning band for red–green colour blindness, which affects about 1 in 12 men (ΔE 6.6, where 8 is the target). Blue and red separate clearly (ΔE 27.0). So in charts gains are blue and losses red. Text keeps the green and red colours, always with a +/− sign or ▲/▼ icon. I added five chart colour tokens to frontend.md §5.2 with the reason, and logged it in CHANGELOG.md.

What the design covers:

PartKey points
Review screenRules table with an Edit link on each line to the right builder step. Period quick choices limited to the data range and to weekly availability. Editable costs with sources. Optional capital. The "I've checked" checkbox clears itself if you change a cost or the period. A "Still needed" list with links.
Evaluate → progressA simple status flow covering retries, the busy message, a 422 back to Review, and Cancel (a late answer is ignored). The time estimate scales with period length and timeframe. Stage messages, three learning cards, and the keep-this-tab-open warning.
Report layoutSummary → risk tiles → result tiles → equity curve with drawdown directly below → "when it worked" tabs → overfitting check → VIX sensitivity → reserved spaces for what-if and suggestions → trades → settings, method, data sources → disclaimer, with a download bar that stays visible while scrolling.
ChartsEquity as a single line, with drawdown as a separate chart directly below (never two y-axes on one chart). Their hover readouts move together. Breakdowns are tables with a small bar per row, so the numbers are always readable as text. Months are a plain year × month grid of signed values. Overfitting is shown as two columns of tiles; sensitivity is a small table. Every chart has a caption and a "Show as table" option.
PDF / CSVPDF contents page by page, with the disclaimer in every footer. The CSV column list, UTF-8 with a byte-order mark so spreadsheet apps show ₹ correctly. File-name rules.
Engine report builderExact formulas for every metric, the seven breakdown groupings and their labels, and the regime rule (from the previous day's ADX). The overfitting decision logic with its five fixed explanation texts. Summary templates, risk first, with a "best/worst" sentence only for groups with at least 10 trades. Texts for all 14 skip reasons, plus the "how this was simulated" lines.

Target %20–150%10
Stop-loss points5–1005
Target points10–20010

Sliders appear for any strategy using these stop-loss or target types, not only templates.

## Plan: "How we plan specs" explainer (one HTML page)

Audience: engineers and leads on other teams, especially those building microservices or micro-frontends (several small web apps that make up one UI). It assumes they have never seen Nikasha.

Length: about a 6–8 minute read. Every section is short, with tables and one diagram instead of long text. Details are in collapsible "show example" boxes, so a reader can skim the page or open the boxes.

### Sections

  1. Why spec first (one paragraph). We agree on the problem, the rules and the APIs before writing code, so AI and humans build the same thing.
  2. The structure at a glance. One layered diagram, from top to bottom:
    – constitution
    – product
    – architecture
    – frontend and backend guides
    – contracts
    – feature folders (requirements, then design, then tasks)
    – traceability and changelog

A table gives each file's one-line purpose and its owner.

  1. How we created it (the process). A six-step timeline:
    – brief
    – clarifying questions (we answered 22)
    – top-down drafting with an approval gate on each file
    – contracts validated by tools
    – feature folders
    – three review rounds, then freeze

It also shows the Nikasha numbers: 7 features, 208 requirements, 137 acceptance criteria and 126 tasks, all traced.

  1. What a feature folder looks like. One real example running through it: an EARS requirement ("When…, the system shall…"), then its acceptance criterion, then the design section, then the task, then the test. Requirement IDs stay fixed and are never reused.
  2. Change process. A small flowchart: is it a behaviour change, a bug fix or a refactor? Only behaviour changes go back through the spec, with a changelog entry and a traceability update.
  3. Advantages. About six bullets:
    – one source of truth
    – frontend and backend can build in parallel against the contracts
    – AI-friendly
    – easier reviews
    – you can trace what is tested
    – safer changes
  4. Using it for microservices and micro-frontends. A mapping table, which is the main point for this audience:
    – one contracts folder per service, holding OpenAPI and event schemas
    – consumer-driven contract tests
    – a backend.md per service, and a frontend.md per micro-frontend
    – a shared design-system spec
    – the constitution and architecture written once at platform level
    – feature folders that cut across services
    – contract versioning
    – a column for which team owns each file
  5. Disadvantages, kept honest and short. I think there are real ones, so I'd keep this section:
    – it takes more time up front
    – the spec can drift from the code without discipline
    – it is too heavy for small tweaks
    – review fatigue
  6. Starter template. A copyable folder tree another team could start from, plus a one-page checklist.

### Format

  • A single self-contained .html file with no external dependencies, that works on a phone and has a dark mode.
  • Nikasha is used only as the worked example, so the method stays general.

### Where it goes

  • I'll save it outside the repo, so nothing is committed, at ~/Desktop/Tarun/IISc Banaglore/spec-structure-guide.html.
  • I can also publish it as a private claude.ai link that you can share with the team if you want. Just tell me.

Shall I go ahead? Also tell me if you'd like any section added, removed or shortened (for example, dropping section 9).

## Revised plan: "How we plan specs" (single local HTML page)

Audience: a 10–15 person microservice and micro-frontend team with frontend, backend, full-stack, QA, DevOps, tech lead and product/BA roles.

Length: about 10 minutes to skim. Details sit in collapsible boxes, so the page stays short.

### Sections

  1. Why spec first. One paragraph.
  1. The structure at a glance. A layered diagram, plus a table giving each file's one-line purpose:
    – constitution
    – product
    – architecture
    – frontend and backend guides
    – contracts
    – feature folders (requirements, then design, then tasks)
    – traceability
    – changelog
  1. How Nikasha's spec was created. A short timeline:
    – brief
    – clarifying questions
    – top-down drafts with an approval gate on each
    – contracts checked by tools
    – feature folders
    – three review rounds

It closes with the counts: 7 features, 208 requirements, 126 tasks, all traced.

  1. Anatomy of one feature. One example traced end to end: requirement (EARS) → acceptance criterion → design → task → test. Requirement IDs are stable and never reused.
  1. New: who writes what in a team of 10–15. A role table:
RoleOwns / writesReviews
Product owner / BAproduct.md, the EARS requirementsacceptance criteria
Tech lead / architectconstitution, architecture, contractseverything
Backend leads (per service)backend spec for their service, its contractconsumer contracts
Frontend leads (per micro-frontend)frontend.md, the shared design-system specthe APIs they call
QAacceptance criteria detail, test column in traceability, fixturesevery requirement for testability
DevOpsdeployment, NFRs, environmentsarchitecture

Also covered:

  • How the team works:
    – spec changes go through pull requests
    – each folder has a designated reviewer, enforced with CODEOWNERS
    – a feature is "ready" when its requirements, contract and acceptance criteria are approved
    – a feature is "done" when its traceability row shows a passing test
    – the spec is frozen per sprint
    – Keeping it parallel: once a contract is approved, frontend, backend and QA all start from it on the same day. Mocks and fixtures come from the contract.
  1. New: sharing specs across repos. A diagram and step-by-step flow:
    – One central spec repo, platform-specs. It holds the constitution, product, architecture, contracts and feature folders.
    – Specs are released with version tags (v1.4.0). Each service repo pins the version it builds against.
    – Contracts are published as packages. Typed clients and models are generated from OpenAPI or event schemas, using npm or PyPI packages or a git submodule.
    – Each service repo keeps a small local spec: a pointer file naming the pinned version, plus that service's own design and task notes.
    – CI in every service repo checks its code against the pinned contracts (contract tests, drift check).
    – Change flow: spec PR → review → tag → providers implement → consumers bump their version.
    – Breaking-change rule: additive changes are a minor version; breaking changes are a major version and need a deprecation window.
  1. New: does this work for MCP servers? Yes, and it fits well.
    – Mapping table:
    – MCP tools, resources and prompts are contracts: JSON Schema for each tool's input and output, written in contracts/.
    – A tool's description is user-facing text, so it gets the same review as UI copy.
    – Tool behaviour is written as EARS requirements, for example: "When get_order is called with an unknown id, the server shall return a not-found error…"
    – The constitution holds the rules on auth, scopes and destructive actions, such as "no write tool without confirmation".
    – Fixtures are recorded tool calls; tests replay them, so no live calls are made.
    – Bonus idea: expose the spec repo itself through a small read-only "spec MCP server". Every developer's coding assistant can then look up requirements, contracts and IDs from any repo.
  1. Change process. A small flowchart: behaviour change, bug fix, or refactor. Only a behaviour change touches the spec.
  1. Advantages. About six bullets.
  1. Disadvantages, honestly.
    – More time up front.
    – Specs drift from code without CI checks.
    – Too heavy for small tweaks.
    – Review fatigue.
    – The central spec repo can become a bottleneck. The fix is to give each folder an owner.
  1. Starter template. A copyable folder tree for the central spec repo and for a service repo, plus a one-page "first week" checklist.

### Format and location

  • One self-contained HTML file with no internet dependencies. It works on mobile and has a dark mode.
  • It will be saved locally at ~/Desktop/Tarun/IISc Banaglore/spec-structure-guide.html, outside the Nikasha repo, with nothing committed and nothing uploaded.
  • Nikasha is the worked example; everything else is kept generic.

Should I go ahead and write it? If the page sounds too long, my first cuts would be section 3 (shortened to a few lines) and section 11 (the folder tree only).

Claude's response

Here's the full path from scratch. Skip Part A if you already have an AWS account.

### Part A: create an AWS account (about 10 minutes)

  1. Go to https://aws.amazon.com and click Create an AWS Account.
  2. Enter your email, an account name (for example "nikasha") and a password.
  3. Choose Personal and fill in your details.
  4. Add a card. AWS needs one even for the free tier, and our budget alert will warn you before any charge.
  5. Verify your phone, then choose the Basic support (free) plan.
  6. Sign in to the console as the root user, with the email and password you just created.

### Part B: create a user for deploying

Avoid using the root account's keys; a separate user is safer.

  1. In the console's top search bar, type IAM and open it.
  2. In the left menu, click Users, then Create user.
  3. Enter the user name nikasha-deploy, then click Next.
  4. Choose Attach policies directly, search for AdministratorAccess, tick it, then click Next and Create user.
    – SAM has to create Lambda functions, roles, logs, container storage and a budget, and this is the simplest permission that covers all of them. You can delete this user after the course.
  5. Click the new user nikasha-deploy and open the Security credentials tab.
  6. Under Access keys, click Create access key.
  7. Choose Command Line Interface (CLI), tick the confirmation box, click Next, then Create access key.
  8. Keep this page open. It shows the Access key ID and the Secret access key, and the secret is shown only once.

### Part C: connect this Mac

  1. Open the Terminal app (Cmd + Space, type "Terminal", press Enter).
  2. Run:
    ``bash
    aws configure
    ``
  3. Answer the four prompts:
    ``
    AWS Access Key ID [None]: ← paste the Access key ID, press Enter
    AWS Secret Access Key [None]: ← paste the Secret access key, press Enter
    Default region name [None]: ap-south-1
    Default output format [None]: json
    ``
  4. Close the AWS page with the keys. They're now saved on your Mac in ~/.aws/credentials.

Don't paste either key into this chat. Reply "done" when Part C is finished, and I'll check the sign-in with a read-only command that only shows which account you're signed in to.

Nikasha is working as designed here. All ten rules it lists depend on something outside what it can backtest today, and the specs put these deliberately out of scope for the first release. The listing matches the reasons fairly:

Rule in your PDFWhy it can't be tested yet
CPR / pivot levels (P, TC, BC, R1, S1, R2, S2)Not one of the supported indicators (EMA, SMA, RSI, ADX, Supertrend, MACD, Bollinger)
Opening-range high/low linesThere's no "first candle's high/low" value to compare against
Call and put entry rulesBoth are built on the CPR and opening-range levels
Exit when a candle closes beyond the 20 EMAExits can only be stop-loss, target, time or candle count, not indicator-based
Move to cost after +25 premium pointsOnly "after a gain of x times the initial risk" is supported
Book 50% at R1/S1, trail the rest to R2/S2Partial exits aren't supported; each trade closes all at once
Wide-CPR filter, 70-point candle-size filterNo such filters exist

What still carries over: the parts Nikasha understands should appear in the "found" and "questions" sections of the same screen:

  • buy options;
  • call on the call signal, put on the put signal;
  • 100 points in the money;
  • weekly expiry;
  • 15-minute timeframe;
  • stop-loss at the trigger candle, capped at 25 premium points;
  • entries only before 11:30;
  • 1 trade per day.

Because both entry rules can't be used, there's no entry trigger left. After you acknowledge the list, the app sends you to the builder's Entry step to set one yourself (logged earlier as R19). On the review screen, these rules appear under "left out", so it's clear the backtest isn't your full strategy.

This is a strategy Nikasha can't faithfully test today. Anything you build in place of the entry rules would be a different strategy, so the result wouldn't tell you how this plan would have done.

If you want support for strategies like this, it's a real feature request and a behaviour change. Roughly in order of effort:

  1. Opening-range high/low as values to compare with (for example, "close above the first 15-minute candle's high").
  2. Daily pivot and CPR levels as indicators, calculated from the previous day's high, low and close. The data supports this.
  3. Indicator-based exits (for example, "exit when a candle closes below EMA 20").
  4. Trailing to cost after a fixed premium-point gain.
  5. Partial exits (50% at one level, the rest trailed). This is the biggest change, because it alters how every trade, report and cost is calculated.

Each one needs a spec proposal, engine work, builder and upload changes, and tests. With deployment planned for tomorrow, I'd recommend shipping as it is and taking these up afterwards.

What would you like to do?

  • (a) Keep the scope and continue testing, then deploy. I'll record these five items as candidate future features in the specs so they aren't lost.
  • (b) Pick one or more items to add now. I'll write the spec proposal first, as the change process requires.

I have enough to write a concrete proposal. Two things need saying first.

  1. Effort and the schedule. These five features touch the contracts, the engine's simulation core, reports, CSV and PDF, the builder, the AI upload reader and all their tests. Done carefully, that's a long build, and partial exits alone change how every trade is simulated, costed and reported. Deployment planned for tomorrow is at real risk. If time runs short, the cut order would be 5 → 3 → 4.
  2. Your strategy needs a few extra pieces beyond my earlier list of five: "exit when spot touches R2/S2", a stop at the breakout candle's high/low, and the CPR width. I've folded them into the features they belong to (marked *).

---

## Proposal: five new capabilities

New requirement IDs continue from the current highest in each feature (ENG-47, INP-21, UPL-21, RPT-21).

### F1. Opening range (new indicator OPENING_RANGE)

  • Parameters: minutes, from 5 to 120 (for example 15).
  • Outputs: high, low. These are the underlying's highest high and lowest low over the first N minutes of each session (09:15 to 09:15+N−1).
  • No looking ahead: a candle sees the day's values only from the candle that closes at or after the range ends. Before that, the value is empty, so any condition using it is false. Values reset each day.
  • Your rules map to:
    – "First candle above CPR" becomes "opening range low > CPR top".
    – "Close above the opening-range high" becomes "close > opening range high". Also add "open > opening range high" if "body completely above" should mean both open and close.

REQ-ENG-48: THE engine SHALL compute OPENING_RANGE high/low per session from the first N minutes and SHALL expose them only on candles closing at or after the range's end.

### F2. Daily pivots and CPR (new indicator PIVOTS)

  • Calculation: classic floor pivots from the previous trading day's session high (H), low (L) and close (C) in the data. The first day in the data has no values.
  • Outputs:
    – p = (H+L+C)/3
    – bc = (H+L)/2, tc = 2P − BC. These are then ordered so that tc ≥ bc.
    – r1 = 2P − L, s1 = 2P − H, r2 = P + (H − L), s2 = P − (H − L), r3 and s3 likewise.
    – cpr_width* = tc − bc, in points.
  • Values are constant for the whole day.
  • Your "wide CPR" filter becomes "CPR width < N points". "Wide" is vague, so the upload flow asks you for N.

REQ-ENG-49: THE engine SHALL compute PIVOTS outputs from the previous trading day's session high, low and close, constant across the day.

### F3. Exit on a condition

  • New exit.exit_when: a condition tree per side (call, put, or all legs), built in the same editor as entry conditions.
  • When it's checked: on each candle close after the entry fill.
  • When it fires: the position exits at the next minute's open, with exit reason SIGNAL_EXIT ("Exit condition met").
  • Tie-break: if a stop or target is hit in that same minute, the condition exit wins, because it fills at the minute's open.
  • Holding styles: works for intraday and positional. For positional strategies it counts as one of the allowed exit choices.

REQ-ENG-50, REQ-INP-22

### F4. Move to cost after a fixed premium gain

  • New option advanced.trail_to_cost_at_pts: premium points, combined per unit for multiple legs.
  • It can't be set together with the existing "× risk" option (new rule SEM-19).
  • In the builder, the trail-to-cost option gets a choice between "× initial risk" and "premium points".

REQ-ENG-51, REQ-INP-23

### F5. Partial exit, then the rest *

advanced.partial_exit settings:

SettingAllowed values
percent1–99. Rounded down to whole lots, with at least 1 lot closed and 1 left. With a single lot it has no effect, and the report counts this
whenPREMIUM_GAIN (% of premium, points, or × risk), or SPOT_LEVEL: the index touches a value (calls: high ≥ level; puts: low ≤ level), such as pivots R1 for calls and S1 for puts
then_stop (for the rest)UNCHANGED, COST, or PARTIAL_CANDLE* (the index breaks back below the low, for calls, or above the high, for puts, of the candle in which the partial exit happened; active once that candle closes)

New target type SPOT_LEVEL\*: exit everything when the index touches a level, such as R2 for calls and S2 for puts.

How these behave:

  • Spot-level options are for single-leg strategies only (new rule SEM-20).
  • Fills happen at the modelled premium at that level, or at the minute's open if the price gapped through it.
  • Costs are charged separately for the partial exit order.
  • A trade then has two exits:
    – the report's trade table, the CSV and the PDF gain "Partial exit time, quantity, premium" columns;
    – the API's Trade gets an optional partial_exit and new exit reasons SIGNAL_EXIT, SPOT_TARGET and TRAILING_STOP.
  • Sizing by India VIX moves to before the simulation, so the lot split is known in advance.

REQ-ENG-52–55, REQ-RPT-22–23, REQ-INP-24

### Across all five

  • Upload (REQ-UPL-22):
    – the AI vocabulary and prompt learn all of the above;
    – the condition rows gain exit sides;
    – every new option fits into the existing settings list and text fields, so the compact AI schema doesn't grow (its headroom is only about 2 fields).
  • Builder:
    – the indicator picker gets "Opening range" and "Daily pivots / CPR";
    – the Exit step gets "Exit when…";
    – Advanced options gets "Partial exit";
    – "Mirror from Call" swaps R↔S, TC↔BC and high↔low.
  • Contracts: strategy.schema.json, api.openapi.yaml and strategy-rules.md (SEM-19, SEM-20), new valid and invalid fixtures, and regenerated code.
  • Tests:
    – engine unit tests with hand-calculated pivots and opening ranges, plus no-lookahead tests;
    – partial-exit costs to the paisa;
    – golden reports unchanged for existing strategies;
    – your ORB + CPR PDF as a new live upload sample, recorded once (a few cents).

Still out of scope: the 70-point candle-size filter. It needs a "candle range" value (high − low), a small sixth item I can add cheaply if you want.

---

Before I start, please confirm:

  1. Approve F1–F5 as written, including the items marked *?
  2. The PARTIAL_CANDLE stop is measured on the index chart, as your PDF says, rather than the option premium chart. OK?
  3. Add the small sixth item (candle range, for the 70-point filter)?
  4. Deployment may move past tomorrow. OK?

79
PROMPT 2026-10-15 15:02 UTC

what is R23 tell me now, I will try to answer now

Claude's response

R23 is about what "when the index reaches R1" means if the index is already past R1 when the trade is entered.

How it works today (the rule as approved)

Nikasha looks at where the level sits when the trade opens:

  • Level above the index at entry: reached when the index rises to it.
  • Level below the index at entry: reached when the index falls to it.

Where this goes wrong, with your ORB strategy

  1. At 09:30 the opening-range breakout fires a call and you buy at 10:00. The index is at 24,600, and today's R1 is 24,550, so the index has already gone past R1.
  2. The rule "book 50% when the index reaches R1" now waits for the index to fall back to 24,550.
  3. If it does, half the position is booked at a loss, which is the opposite of what "profit booking at R1" means.

The same applies to the R2 target. If the index is already above R2 at entry, the "target" would only trigger on a fall back to R2. In the 2024–25 test run, some of the 9 partial exits were like this.

Your options

Rule for a level already passed at entryEffect in the example above
1. Keep as isReached when the index comes back to itBooks 50% at a loss if the index falls back to 24,550
2. Never reached (my recommendation)A level for calls only counts while it's above the index (puts: below). If it's already passed, that trade simply doesn't use itNo partial exit and no R2 target for that trade; the stop, trail, EMA exit and 15:15 square-off still apply
3. Reached at onceTreated as already hit, so it executes at the first minute after entryBooks 50% (or the full target) right after entering, at roughly the entry price

Why I recommend option 2: a profit level only makes sense on the profitable side, and option 2 never turns "profit booking" into a loss. Option 3 creates near-pointless trades that exit straight after entry.

One more detail: whichever option you choose applies to every index-level rule (the target, the partial exit and its trailing rest), for calls and puts alike, and the report would show it under "Rules with no effect" when a level is skipped for a trade.

Which do you choose: 1, 2 or 3?

PROMPT 2026-10-15 15:04 UTC

lets go with 2

Claude's response · 12 actions

Option 2 it is. That changes approved behaviour (REQ-ENG-53), so the order is: update the spec wording (the requirement keeps its ID), then code, then tests.

The rule I'll write: a level counts only if, when the trade starts, it lies on the trade's profitable side:

  • above the index for trades that gain when the index rises (bought calls, sold puts);
  • below the index for trades that gain when it falls (bought puts, sold calls).

Otherwise that rule isn't used for that trade, and the report counts it under "Rules with no effect". Starting with the spec:

  • Ran a command: Spec update for R23 option 2

Now the engine. The level tracker will use the trade's profitable direction, and a level already passed is recorded on the trade path so the runner can count it as "no effect":

What changed

  • An index level (the R2 target, or the R1 partial exit) is used only if, when the trade starts, it's on the trade's profitable side: above the index for bought calls, below for bought puts.
  • If it's already been passed, that rule is simply not used for that trade. The stop, the move to cost, the EMA exit and the 15:15 square-off still apply.
  • The report lists each skipped level under "Rules with no effect" with the number of trades, so it's never hidden.

I'll go through the open items in specs/PENDING-REVIEW.md one at a time. Each is a reading I chose where the spec left a detail open. Most are small, so for each I'll explain it plainly and give a recommendation; you can answer "keep", or choose otherwise.

  • Ran a command: Count pending items and read R1

There are 26 open items (R23 is already decided). Here's the first.

R1 of 26: Which error message shows when a time window is entered backwards

If someone types an entry window backwards, for example "from 14:00 to 10:00", two rules could complain:

  • "a window must start before it ends";
  • "your windows leave no time to enter a trade".

What I did: show only the first message, because it names the real mistake; the second one would be confusing noise. Separately, an "expiry-day cutoff" (no new trades after, say, 12:00 on expiry day) never triggers "no time to enter a trade", because it only affects expiry days and every other day still has time.

Recommendation: keep. It shows the clearest message.

Keep, or would you prefer something else?

Claude's response · 1 action

R1 is recorded as approved.

R2 of 26: Two messages for the same mistake

If a strategy is set to Intraday but also says "hold until expiry", two rules catch it, and the review screen shows both messages:

  • "Intraday positions close by 15:15, so this exit can't apply."
  • "This exit only applies to positional strategies."

They say the same thing two ways. In practice this is rare: the builder only offers "hold until expiry" for positional strategies, so it mostly arises from an uploaded document.

Recommendation: keep. Both messages are true, the engine and the website agree on them through a shared test file, and changing it means touching both validators for a case users will rarely see. If you'd rather show just one, I'd keep the second, which is clearer.

Keep, or show just one?

R3 of 26: Entry rules for multi-leg strategies (such as a straddle)

A multi-leg strategy opens all its legs together, for example selling a call and a put at the same moment. The spec says such a strategy enters "at a fixed time and/or when one shared set of conditions is met".

What I did: a multi-leg strategy can't have separate call conditions and put conditions, because the legs always open together, so there's only one set of conditions for all of them. The builder already works this way and the website and engine agree.

Recommendation: keep. Separate call and put conditions would mean the legs open at different times. That's a different kind of strategy, not supported in this version.

Keep?
